Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example4/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/gres.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise4/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise1/run_4.sbatch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon, use the `--gpus` flag (or the older `--gres=gpu:1`) along with the appropriate GPU partition. Here is a minimal example: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=my_gpu_job #SBATCH --output=%x_%j.out #SBATCH --error=%x_%j.err #SBATCH --gpus=1 # Request 1 GPU #SBATCH --time=01:00:00 # Adjust time as needed #SBATCH --mem=8G # Adjust memory as needed #SBATCH --partition=gpu # Use the GPU partition # Your command here python my_script.py ``` **Key points:** * **`--gpus=1`**: Requests one GPU. You can also use `--gres=gpu:1` if your cluster configuration requires it. * **`--partition=gpu`**: Ensures your job is scheduled on a GPU-enabled node. * **Memory & Time**: Adjust `--mem` and `--time` to match your job's needs. For more details on SLURM GPU options, see the [Slurm Workload Manager – sbatch documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 3: question_3

❓ Question

How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you don't specify a memory request, SLURM defaults to **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). This is intentionally small, so most jobs will fail with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error unless you explicitly request more. To set a higher limit, add to your job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the memory in MB (e.g., `--mem 4G` for 4 GB) [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
If you don't specify a memory request, SLURM defaults to **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). This is intentionally small, so most jobs will fail with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error unless you explicitly request more. To set a higher limit, add to your job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the memory in MB (e.g., `--mem 4G` for 4 GB) [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).

How much storage does a FASRC lab group directory get, and how many files?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
A FASRC lab directory (`/n/holylabs/_lab`) gets **4 TB** of storage and is **not expandable** [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage). The documentation does not specify a file-count limit for lab directories. For details on retention and other options, see the [Data Storage Workflow](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Each lab gets a 4 TiB lab directory as a hard limit, with a 1 million file limit.

Question 9: question_9

❓ Question

What command shows my lab's fairshare information on FASRC?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/dual-lab-affiliations-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-stats, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/fair_tree.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sshare.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/.github/workflows/Languages-Python-Example1.yml, slurm.schedmd.com/namespace.yaml.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbcast.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, slurm.schedmd.com/strigger.html, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/WRF_Model/README.md, github.com/OSC/ood-documentation/blob/release-4.1/source/conf.py, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/wckey.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/qos.html, slurm.schedmd.com/network.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_exit_code.html, slurm.schedmd.com/overview.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To check your lab's FairShare score, run: ```bash sshare -u RCUSERNAME ``` Replace `RCUSERNAME` with your FASRC username. The last column in the output shows your effective FairShare score. As a rule of thumb: - **≤ 0.5**: lower priority - **≥ 0.5**: higher priority FairShare affects how quickly your jobs are scheduled — the more you've used the cluster recently, the lower your score temporarily becomes, which can slow scheduling until you've had a chance to use fewer resources. For more details, see the [FairShare documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account= -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Args: fairshare
🤖 Assistant Message #3

For more details, see the [FairShare documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).

Question 10: question_10

❓ Question

How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The home directories have daily snapshots, and you can access them via the hidden `.snapshot` directory in your home. Hourly, daily, and weekly snapshots are available within that directory. Note that if you delete your entire home directory, you will also delete the snapshots, and that is not recoverable.
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Args: How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The home directories have daily snapshots, and you can access them via the hidden `.snapshot` directory in your home. Hourly, daily, and weekly snapshots are available within that directory. Note that if you delete your entire home directory, you will also delete the snapshots, and that is not recoverable.

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here’s how to find out how much memory your job used and how to request more next time: ### 1. Find out how much memory your job actually used - **If your job completed successfully** (not `OUT_OF_MEMORY`), Slurm recorded the true peak memory. You can see it with: ```bash sacct -j YOUR_JOB_ID --format=JobID,MaxRSS,AllocCPUS,State ``` or more conveniently with **`jobstats`**: ```bash jobstats YOUR_JOB_ID ``` This shows memory usage for both running and completed jobs ([Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/)). - **If your job was killed with `OUT_OF_MEMORY`**, the memory usage Slurm recorded is **inaccurate**. In that case, you’ll need to estimate based on what worked in a successful run (see below). ### 2. Request more memory for your next job Add a `--mem` directive to your `#SBATCH` script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` This requests 4 GB per node. You can also use MB (e.g., `--mem 4000`). The larger the value, the harder it may be for the scheduler to find a node, so start with a reasonable estimate and refine ([Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/)). ### 3. How to determine the right amount 1. **Run a test job** with a generous memory request (e.g., `--mem 4G` or more). 2. **Check actual usage** with `jobstats YOUR_JOB_ID` after it completes. 3. **Set `--mem`** to something slightly larger than what `jobstats` reports, since `--mem` is a hard limit. For multi-node jobs, `--mem` is the maximum memory on **any single node**, so ensure your tasks are evenly distributed (e.g., with `--ntasks-per-node`) for consistent results ([Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/)).
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.

Question 12: question_12

❓ Question

Is the two-factor code for logging into FASRC the same as my Harvard Key code? If not, how do I set it up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the two-factor code for logging into FASRC is **not** the same as your Harvard Key code. FASRC uses its own separate OpenAuth two-factor authentication token, distinct from your Harvard Key. However, if you already have the **Duo Mobile** app installed on your phone, you can use it to generate your FASRC two-factor code alongside your Harvard Key token. Here is how to set it up: ### 1. Get the QR Code From a computer or tablet (not your phone): 1. Visit [https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and log in with your FASRC username and password. 2. Check your email for a link sent by the site and click it. 3. This will display a **QR code** for your FASRC OpenAuth token. Leave this page open. ### 2. Add to Duo Mobile On your mobile device: 1. Open the **Duo Mobile** app and tap the **+** icon to add a new token. 2. Use your device's camera to scan the QR code displayed on your computer screen. 3. Once scanned, Duo will save the token. You can now view your 6-digit FASRC two-factor code in the Duo app whenever you need to log in to the cluster or connect to the RC VPN. For more details, see the guide on [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC has its own two-factor system (OpenAuth) that is separate from Harvard Key. Set it up with a smartphone authenticator app (e.g. Google Authenticator or Duo) before accessing cluster resources; you use this OpenAuth token along with your FASRC username and password.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To find available versions and load them reliably on Cannon: ### 1. Find available versions - **`module avail `** — lists versions available in your current environment. Example: `module avail python` - **`module spider `** — shows all versions across the hierarchy, including those that require specific compilers or MPI stacks. Example: `module spider openmpi` - **`module --default avail`** — shows the full module tree regardless of what’s currently loaded, useful when `module avail` is too narrow. ### 2. Load a specific version (recommended) Always specify the full module name, including version and build tag, to avoid breakage when defaults change: ```bash module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01 module load gcc/15.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.8-fasrc01 ``` As FASRC documentation states, “It is **strongly recommended** to load modules specifying name and specific version. This way you are protected from default changes” [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### 3. Verify what’s loaded ```bash module list ``` ### 4. Save/restore module collections (optional) ```bash module save myworkflow # save current set module restore myworkflow # restore later ``` If you tell me the package name, I can show you the exact versions available and the best one to pin.
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load /) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cursor-remote-development-via-ssh-and-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a faster drop-in replacement for Conda) inside a **virtual environment**. Here's the workflow: 1. **Load the Python module** (which includes Mamba): ```bash module load python ``` 2. **Start an interactive session** before creating or modifying environments: ```bash salloc --partition test --nodes=1 --cpus-per-task=2 --mem=4GB --time=0-02:00:00 ``` 3. **Create an environment** (default location is `~/.conda/envs`): ```bash mamba create -n my_env python=3.10 ``` You can also install packages at creation time: ```bash mamba create -n my_env python=3.10 numpy pandas ``` 4. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate my_env ``` 5. **Install additional packages** as needed: ```bash mamba install -y package_name ``` 6. **Deactivate** when done: ```bash source deactivate ``` **Important notes:** - Direct use of `pip` outside a virtual environment is discouraged on FASRC clusters [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation). - The `python` module is built with Miniforge3 and uses `conda-forge` as the default channel, since the default Anaconda channels are blocked on the cluster due to licensing restrictions [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation). - If you want to store packages in a Lab shared directory instead of your home directory, set `CONDA_ENVS_PATH` and `CONDA_PKGS_DIRS` to your desired paths before creating the environment [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation).
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
### How Job Priority Is Determined on Cannon On Cannon, job priority is calculated using Slurm's **Multifactor Priority Plugin**, which combines two main components: 1. **FairShare Score:** This is the primary driver of priority. Every lab (account) has a "Share" of the cluster based on hardware purchased and a base allocation. The FairShare score is calculated based on how much of that Share the account has actually used. Accounts that have used less than their Share receive a higher FairShare score, which translates to higher job priority. This score evolves dynamically over time as usage changes [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare). 2. **Job Age:** Jobs that have been waiting in the pending queue longer accrue additional priority over time. This ensures that jobs don't wait indefinitely if higher-priority jobs keep arriving. The final priority is an integer derived from the FairShare score (multiplied by a weighting factor of 10,000,000 for resolution) plus the accrued Job Age [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare). ### How to See the Priority of Your Pending Jobs You can view the priority of your pending jobs using the `squeue` command with the `--priority` flag. To see your pending jobs sorted by priority (highest first), run: ```bash squeue -u $USER --states=PD --sort=-p ``` * `-u $USER`: Filters output to your jobs. * `--states=PD`: Shows only pending jobs. * `--sort=-p`: Sorts by priority in descending order (highest priority first). You can also add the `-o` option to format the output and explicitly show the priority column: ```bash squeue -u $USER --states=PD -o "%.10i %.8P %.10J %.8u %.2t %.10M %.6D %P %p" ``` In this output, the last column (`%p`) represents the job's priority. For more details on `squeue` options, see the [Slurm squeue documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html).
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 16: question_16

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/transferring-data-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's the short answer: ### Should you use Globus? **Yes, for very large datasets.** Globus is specifically recommended as an alternative to SSH/SCP for transfers that copy very large data sets [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Can you transfer directly to your home directory? **No.** Home directories are not available through Globus under any circumstances. Sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability, as authentication keys/passwords may be shared inadvertently [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Where should you transfer instead? You'll need to transfer to one of your lab shares or other supported storage: - **Lab directories** (`/n/holylabs`) — 4TB, no retention limit - **Netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`) — 50TB, 90-day retention - Other paid storage options (Compute Storage, Lab Storage, etc.) Once the data is in your lab share or scratch, you can use it from the cluster. If you need to move it into your home directory afterward, you can do so via `scp`, `sftp`, or `rsync` from a cluster node.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

🤖 Assistant Message #4
Here's the short answer: ### Should you use Globus? **Yes, for very large datasets.** Globus is specifically recommended as an alternative to SSH/SCP for transfers that copy very large data sets [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Can you transfer directly to your home directory? **No.** Home directories are not available through Globus under any circumstances. Sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability, as authentication keys/passwords may be shared inadvertently [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Where should you transfer instead? You'll need to transfer to one of your lab shares or other supported storage: - **Lab directories** (`/n/holylabs`) — 4TB, no retention limit - **Netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`) — 50TB, 90-day retention - Other paid storage options (Compute Storage, Lab Storage, etc.) Once the data is in your lab share or scratch, you can use it from the cluster. If you need to move it into your home directory afterward, you can do so via `scp`, `sftp`, or `rsync` from a cluster node.

Question 18: question_18

❓ Question

I want to run a short GPU job on Cannon. How do I request the GPU and make sure it has enough memory so it isn't killed?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/troubleshoot.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise2/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To run a short GPU job on Cannon and avoid being killed for exceeding memory limits, you need to explicitly request both a GPU and sufficient memory in your SLURM job script or interactive allocation. ### 1. Request the GPU Use the `--gres=gpu:N` flag, where `N` is the number of GPUs you need. You also need to specify a GPU partition (e.g., `gpu` or `gpu_test`) using the `-p` flag. ### 2. Request Enough Memory To prevent your job from being killed by the Out-Of-Memory (OOM) killer, you must request enough RAM. You can do this in two ways: * **Total Memory:** Use `--mem=SIZE` (e.g., `--mem=32G`) to request a total amount of memory for the entire job. * **Per-CPU Memory:** Use `--mem-per-cpu=SIZE` (e.g., `--mem-per-cpu=8G`) to request memory per CPU core. **Example Interactive Allocation:** ```bash salloc --partition gpu_test --gres=gpu:1 --mem-per-cpu 2G -c 4 --time 01:00:00 ``` This requests 1 GPU, 4 CPU cores, 2GB of memory per core (8GB total), and a 1-hour time limit [Ollama on FASRC Clusters](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md). **Example Batch Script (`job.sbatch`):** ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH -N 1 # 1 node #SBATCH --ntasks-per-node=1 # 1 task #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 # Request 1 GPU #SBATCH -p gpu # Use the GPU partition #SBATCH --mem=32G # Request 32GB total memory #SBATCH -t 00:30:00 # 30 minutes time limit #SBATCH -J my_gpu_job # Job name # Run your GPU application here ``` ### Key Tips to Avoid OOM Kills * **Check your memory needs:** If your application uses a lot of RAM (e.g., loading large models or datasets), increase the `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu` value. * **Use `--mem-per-cpu` wisely:** If you request many CPU cores, `--mem-per-cpu` can add up quickly. Ensure the total memory requested is sufficient for your application's peak usage. * **Monitor usage:** You can check your job's memory usage after it runs using tools like `sstat` or by checking the job's output logs for any OOM-related errors. For more details on SLURM options, you can refer to the [Slurm Workload Manager FAQ](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Request the GPU with #SBATCH --gpus=1. Don't rely on the default memory — if you omit a memory request you only get 100 MB and the job will likely be killed, so set #SBATCH --mem=G to a value your job needs. Submit to a GPU-capable partition and keep within its time limit (for short tests, use a test/short partition).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

🤖 archi's Answer

To submit a job on TACC Frontera respecting its queue limits, you would typically: 1. **Check available partitions and their limits** using `sinfo` to see partition names, node counts, and time limits. 2. **Use `sbatch`** with appropriate flags like `--partition`, `--nodes`, `--ntasks`, `--time`, and `--mem` to match the partition's constraints. 3. **Review TACC's documentation** for specific queue limits, as partitions like `normal`, `large`, `xlarge`, and `development` have different node counts, memory, and time restrictions. For accurate, up-to-date information on Frontera's queue structure and limits, please consult the [TACC Frontera User Documentation](https://portal.tacc.utexas.edu/tacc-guides/frontera) or contact TACC support directly.

That's outside FASRC's documentation — Frontera is a TACC system at UT Austin with its own scheduler policies and queues. Check TACC's user guide for Frontera. I can help with the equivalent on FASRC Cannon (SLURM partitions, limits, and submit scripts) if that's useful.

Question 22: question_22

❓ Question

I'm Harvard faculty but I just want to run jobs inside a colleague's existing lab group on the cluster — I don't want my own group and I don't want to deal with billing. Which job title should I pick on the account request form?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mcz-staff-compute

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You should select **POSTDOC/RESEARCHER** on the account request form. According to the FASRC documentation, "Harvard faculty who are simply collaborating with another PI should also use this designation to avoid triggering billing and vetting required for a new cluster lab group" [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). Selecting "FACULTY" would trigger the requirements for creating your own lab group, including billing and vetting processes, which you want to avoid. By choosing "POSTDOC/RESEARCHER," you can join your colleague's existing lab group as a collaborator without incurring those additional administrative and financial requirements.
✅ Expected Answer
Pick POSTDOC/RESEARCHER. A Harvard faculty member who wants to sign up as an individual collaborator under another faculty member's cluster group should choose POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, because choosing FACULTY requires billing information and starts the new lab setup process (and FACULTY signups require vetting, so they take longer to process).

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Question 23: question_23

❓ Question

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply **their own 33-digit billing string(s)** at signup, which an ordinary researcher (who signs up as POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, GRAD STUDENT, etc.) does not need to provide. Specifically, the signup guidelines state: > "If you have a non-faculty appointment but have PI rights with a supported affiliation and your own 33-digit billing string(s), you should select NON-FACULTY PI (your account and PI rights will need to be vetted before approval. FASRC may contact you for more information)." [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) Additionally, non-faculty PI accounts require **vetting before approval**, whereas ordinary researchers do not.
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.

Question 25: question_25

❓ Question

If a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's cluster account, who ends up paying for that person's compute time, and what happens to their access if the PI leaves?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/), here's what happens: **Billing:** There is no up-front cost for the outside collaborator's account, but the collaborator's compute usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and **billed to the Chan School on a quarterly basis**. **If the PI leaves:** The collaborator's access **ends** unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing. It is the sponsoring PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn. To set this up, the collaborator must choose **'EXTERNAL'** in the account request and note that they are an outside collaborator, and the account must be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account.
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.

Question 27: question_27

❓ Question

Do Harvard Chan people still get charged that yearly per-person fee just to have a Research Computing account?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Good news — **no, the yearly per-person fee is no longer charged.** According to the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview/): > The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is *no longer* used as of the fiscal year 2019. Standard services, including accounts and access to the FAS RC high-performance computing cluster, will be effectively free to all Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI. These FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. So as long as you're affiliated with an eligible PI at the Chan School, you can get an FAS Research Computing account and cluster access at no direct per-person charge. **Note:** Supplemental resources like increased storage or VMs may still involve additional charges — see the [Chan School Billing FAQ](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/harvard-school-of-public-health/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa) for details.
✅ Expected Answer
No. The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is no longer used as of fiscal year 2019. Standard services — including accounts and access to the FASRC high-performance computing cluster — are effectively free to Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI, because these FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. Supplemental resources such as increased storage and VMs still involve additional charges.

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you can't just copy-paste the Cannon Remote-SSH setup to FASSE. The Cannon configuration uses a specific login host (`cannon`) and a `ProxyCommand` that tunnels SSH through Cannon's login node to a compute node via `salloc` and `nc`. FASSE has a different login infrastructure, so that exact `ProxyCommand` won't work. However, you **can** set up VS Code on FASSE compute nodes using the same two general approaches: ### Approach 1: Remote-SSH (similar to Cannon, but with FASSE-specific config) You'd configure your `~/.ssh/config` on your local machine to SSH through FASSE's login host to a compute node, using a similar `ProxyCommand` pattern with `salloc` and `nc`. The key differences are: - Replace `cannon` with FASSE's login host (e.g., `fasselogin01` or similar) - Adjust the `salloc` directives (partition, resources) to match FASSE's SLURM configuration - The compute node's hostname will be different ### Approach 2: Remote Tunnel (interactive job) This approach works on any cluster and doesn't require SSH tunneling: 1. SSH to FASSE's login node 2. Start an interactive SLURM job on a compute node 3. Download and run the VS Code CLI on the compute node 4. Create a remote tunnel from the compute node The tunnel approach is often simpler because you don't need to configure SSH proxy commands. Would you like the specific steps for setting up either approach on FASSE?
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 29: question_29

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC provides **Podman** (not Docker) on the cluster, and the Dev Containers extension needs to be pointed at the Podman socket instead of looking for a Docker daemon. Here's how to configure it: ### 1. Start the Podman system service on the compute node In a VS Code terminal on the cluster, run: ```bash nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 & ``` This starts a Podman API service listening on a Unix socket (typically `/tmp/containers-user-$(id -u)/podman/podman.sock`). ### 2. Update your `devcontainer.json` Add or modify these settings in your devcontainer configuration: ```json { "remoteUser": "root", "mounts": [ "source=/tmp/podman-run-/podman/podman.sock,target=/var/run/docker-host.sock,type=bind" ], "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" }, "workspaceFolder": "${localWorkspaceFolder}", "workspaceMount": "source=${localWorkspaceFolder},target=${workspaceFolder}" } ``` **Important:** Replace `` with your actual user ID (e.g., `1234`). You can find it with `id -u`. ### 3. Set the Docker socket path You may also need to set the `DOCKER_HOST` environment variable inside the container to point to the mounted socket: ```json "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_HOST": "unix:///var/run/docker-host.sock", "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" } ``` ### Why this works FASRC doesn't run a Docker daemon on compute nodes. Instead, it provides **Podman**, which is Docker-compatible but runs in user space without requiring root or a daemon. By starting the `podman system service` and mounting its socket into the dev container, the Dev Containers extension can use its Docker-compatible API to build and run containers. For more details, see the [VS Code Remote Development guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/) and the [Podman documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman/).
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-/podman/podman.sock', replacing with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example2/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to export: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` Starting with TensorFlow 2.16.1, Keras is bundled as version 3.0, which requires this environment variable to be set before importing Keras. You can add it to your conda environment's activation script so it's set automatically each time you activate the environment: ```bash mkdir -p $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d echo 'export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"' >> $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d/env_vars.sh ``` See the [TensorFlow installation guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow/) for the full setup steps.
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, the GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the card into smaller instances. To use a full GPU instance, you need to explicitly tell PyTorch which MIG device to use by setting the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable. Here's what to do: 1. **Find the MIG device name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set the environment variable to the MIG instance:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d ``` (Replace with the actual MIG identifier from your `nvidia-smi -L` output.) 3. **Or automate it:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This step is **only required on the `gpu_test` partition** — it's not necessary on the regular `gpu` partition. Source: [PyTorch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/)
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname is set in the CryoSPARC Master configuration file: **`cryosparc_master/config.sh`** Specifically, look for the `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` variable. For example: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` You'll need to: 1. Edit `cryosparc_master/config.sh` and set `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` to the correct hostname of the node where the master is running. 2. Restart the master so it picks up the change: ```shell cryosparcm fixdbport cryosparcm restart ``` This is documented in the FASRC User_Codes README for CryoSPARC [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md).
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/run_alphafold_multi.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the [AlphaFold documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/), **do not** leave the weights in `netscratch`. The instructions are explicit: > Google will provide a file `file_name.bin.zst`. Extract with `unzstd file.bin.zst`. Place `file.bin` in a **lab share** (do not put in netscratch) — this will be the location of your `--model_dir`. ### Why not netscratch? `netscratch` is temporary, high-performance scratch space that is **not backed up** and files older than 90 days will be purged ([Scratch Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/)). The AlphaFold 3 model weights are large, valuable, and needed for every run — they belong in a lab share where they'll persist. ### What to do: 1. **Unpack** the weights: ```bash unzstd file_name.bin.zst ``` 2. **Move** the extracted `file.bin` to your lab share (e.g., `/n/holylabs/...` or wherever your lab's persistent storage is). 3. **Point** AlphaFold 3 to that location via the `--model_dir` flag when you run inference. The AlphaFold 3 database itself is already available cluster-wide at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3`, so you don't need to download that separately.
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.

Question 38: question_38

❓ Question

I have about 12,000 near-identical runs to do. Can I submit them as one --array job, and does that eat into the number of jobs I'm allowed to have queued?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/rest_api.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
1) **Summary of Attempt**: The agent attempted to determine if submitting 12,000 jobs as a single SLURM array job is feasible and whether it counts against the user's job queue limit. It searched for SLURM documentation on job arrays, FASRC-specific limits on queued jobs, and the `MaxArraySize` configuration parameter. 2) **Answer**: - **Yes, you can use `--array`**: SLURM supports job arrays, allowing you to submit many similar jobs with a single command (e.g., `sbatch --array=0-11999 script.sh`). This is the standard way to handle large numbers of independent tasks. - **Queue Limit Impact**: A job array is typically counted as **one** job in the queue status (e.g., `squeue`), but it consumes resources for each task. However, FASRC (and many clusters) impose a **MaxArraySize** limit to prevent a single array from overwhelming the system. You must check if `MaxArraySize` on your cluster is at least 12,000. If it is lower, you will need to split your work into multiple array submissions or use a job array manager. - **Resource Usage**: While it counts as one job ID, each array task consumes a slot in the scheduler's accounting. Ensure your resource requests (CPU, memory, time) are appropriate for each individual task to avoid holding up resources unnecessarily. 3) **Note**: This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
💬 Agent Messages (1)
🤖 Assistant Message #1
1) **Summary of Attempt**: The agent attempted to determine if submitting 12,000 jobs as a single SLURM array job is feasible and whether it counts against the user's job queue limit. It searched for SLURM documentation on job arrays, FASRC-specific limits on queued jobs, and the `MaxArraySize` configuration parameter. 2) **Answer**: - **Yes, you can use `--array`**: SLURM supports job arrays, allowing you to submit many similar jobs with a single command (e.g., `sbatch --array=0-11999 script.sh`). This is the standard way to handle large numbers of independent tasks. - **Queue Limit Impact**: A job array is typically counted as **one** job in the queue status (e.g., `squeue`), but it consumes resources for each task. However, FASRC (and many clusters) impose a **MaxArraySize** limit to prevent a single array from overwhelming the system. You must check if `MaxArraySize` on your cluster is at least 12,000. If it is lower, you will need to split your work into multiple array submissions or use a job array manager. - **Resource Usage**: While it counts as one job ID, each array task consumes a slot in the scheduler's accounting. Ensure your resource requests (CPU, memory, time) are appropriate for each individual task to avoid holding up resources unnecessarily. 3) **Note**: This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your job takes about 3 hours on the shared partition, you should request **9 hours** on `serial_requeue`. The `serial_requeue` partition can place your job on substantially slower hardware, and a good rule of thumb is to allow for a factor of three variance in speed [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/).
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/geophysics-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/huce-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The short answer is: **Fairshare is a global metric, not a per-partition one.** Even if you only run jobs on your lab’s private partition, you are still charged against your group’s global fairshare balance. Here’s why: * **Global Fairshare:** Fairshare is calculated globally across the entire cluster. "RawShares" are granted to your lab group based on the hardware they purchased, but these shares can be used anywhere on the cluster. Because fairshare is a global quantity, your usage is tracked regardless of which partition you use ([Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/)). * **Charges Apply Everywhere:** When you run a job, your group is charged for the resources used (CPU, memory, GPU) against your account's share balance. This happens even if the job is running on a partition your PI specifically paid for ([Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/)). * **Hardware vs. Usage:** While purchasing hardware increases your group's "RawShares" (your baseline allocation), your actual fairshare score is a ratio of your usage relative to everyone else. If your group uses a lot of resources (even on your own nodes), your fairshare can drop if other groups are using less or if your usage exceeds the baseline provided by your hardware purchase. In short, owning the hardware gives your group a higher starting "RawShare" allocation, but it doesn't exempt you from the global fairshare accounting system. If your fairshare is dropping, it means your group's resource consumption is being weighted heavily in the global calculation.
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 41: question_41

❓ Question

Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_state_codes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, slurm.schedmd.com/qos.html, slurm.schedmd.com/rest_api.html, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what we know about how wasted compute affects your lab's score: ### How FairShare Score Works Your lab's **FairShare score** determines job scheduling priority — the more compute your lab consumes, the lower the score becomes, temporarily [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ) – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/). You can check your current score by running: ```bash sshare -u RCUSERNAME ``` The "effective score" appears in the last column, where a lower value indicates lower priority. As a rule of thumb, scores ≤ 0.5 indicate higher priority [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ) – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/). ### Does Wasted Compute Count? The documentation indicates that FairShare usage is based on **actual compute consumed** (CPU hours). However, the indexed documentation does not explicitly detail: 1. **Whether jobs that die from node failures** (which are automatically requeued by SLURM [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/)) count toward your score 2. **Whether cancelled jobs** (manually cancelled due to misconfiguration) count toward your score 3. **The exact decay rate** or timeline for how quickly your score recovers after compute is consumed ### What We Know About Decay From the SLURM documentation, resource usage (including FairShare-related metrics) is **decayed over time** according to a parameter called `PriorityDecayHalfLife` [Slurm Workload Manager - Resource Limits](https://slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html). This means usage doesn't stay at full weight indefinitely — it gradually decreases. ### Recommendation For specific details on whether failed/cancelled jobs count and the exact decay timeline for your cluster, I'd recommend contacting [RCHelp](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) directly, as this appears to be cluster-specific configuration that may not be fully documented in the public knowledge base.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

❓ Question

I'm a master's student and my advisor wants me to run analyses on the Nielsen consumer panel data. Can I be added to the agreement?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the [Nielsen Dataset documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset/), **no, you cannot be added to the agreement as a master's student.** The Master Access Agreement explicitly states that data is available only to: * PhD students * Postdocs * Tenure-track faculty registered on the Nielsen site The documentation specifically notes: **"Data is not available to undergraduates or masters students."** Your advisor (the Principal Investigator) is responsible for ensuring compliance with the Data Use Agreement, which also restricts data disclosure to "PhD seeking students of Harvard working under the supervision or direction of Researcher." If you need to work with this data, you may need to explore alternative data sources or collaborate with PhD students or faculty who are already authorized under the agreement.

No. Under the Master Access Agreement, the Nielsen data and license are made solely to the individual researcher registered via the Chicago Booth Nielsen site. That researcher can be a Ph.D. student or a postdoc who has a tenure-track faculty member registered on the site — but the data is explicitly not available to undergraduates or master's students. Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.

Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-figure-out-how-efficient-my-job-is, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/training-materials, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, slurm.schedmd.com/rest.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are significant issues with this approach. **FASRC's Web Scraping Policy explicitly discourages using the cluster for scraping.** As stated in their documentation: > "We *highly* *discourage* using the cluster itself to scrape data. Due to its size and ease of parallelization of processes, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers. Please seek another avenue for data acquisition first." [Web Scraping Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy) **Key concerns:** 1. **You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity** using the cluster. 2. **The target website's terms of service still apply** — being academic research does not exempt you from their usage policies. 3. **IP blacklisting and legal action** are real risks when scraping at scale, especially from a university cluster that shares IP ranges with other researchers. 4. **You could negatively affect other researchers** who share the same IP space or infrastructure. **Recommended approach:** - Scrape the data elsewhere (e.g., on your local machine, a cloud instance, or a dedicated scraping service) and then bring the data to Cannon for analysis. - If you must scrape from the cluster, contact FASRC first for guidance. - If scraping is for training a generative AI model, contact Harvard's Office of the General Counsel or Office of the Vice Provost for Research. Would you like help with alternative approaches to your research workflow?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.

Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your account is likely not misconfigured—this is expected behavior based on how FASRC storage is structured for Globus: 1. **Home directory not visible in Globus:** Globus on FASRC systems is typically configured to expose specific shared directories (like your lab's working directory) rather than your personal home directory. This is a common security and performance practice. You may need to access your home directory via SSH/SCP or through the FASRC web interface instead. 2. **"Everyone" folder not showing in Globus:** According to FASRC documentation, the **Everyone** directory is explicitly **not available on Globus**. It is intended only for internal sharing on the HPC cluster itself. Globus only exposes the **Lab** directory, which is the primary working directory shared out via Globus [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). **What you can do:** - Use the **Lab** folder in Globus for file transfers with external collaborators - Access the **Everyone** folder only when logged into the HPC cluster directly (via SSH or Open OnDemand) - If you need to access your home directory, use SSH/SCP or the Open OnDemand file manager instead If you're still having issues accessing your Lab folder in Globus, you may need to verify that your Globus endpoint is properly configured. You can check the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer) documentation for setup details.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 46: question_46

❓ Question

I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For a directory with ~500,000 small files, the recommended approach is to **create one or more `tar` archives** before transferring via Globus. Transferring hundreds of thousands of individual files is extremely slow due to per-file overhead, and tar archives consolidate everything into fewer, larger files that Globus can move much more efficiently. ### Recommended steps 1. **Create a file list** (optional but useful for record-keeping): ```bash cd /path/to/your/data find mydir/ -type f > file-list.txt ``` 2. **Create a checksum file** to verify integrity later: ```bash find mydir/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > checksums.shasum ``` 3. **Create one or more tar archives**. For very large datasets, break them into smaller archives (e.g., 50–200 GB each) to reduce the risk of total data loss from corruption: ```bash tar -czf mydir.tar.gz mydir/ ``` If the data is too large for a single archive, split it by subdirectories or use `split` after creating the tar. 4. **Transfer the tar archive(s) via Globus** to your destination. ### Why this approach? - **Performance**: Tar reduces hundreds of thousands of individual file operations to a handful of large-file transfers, which is far more efficient over Globus. - **Integrity**: Checksums let you verify that the data arrived intact. - **Safety**: Breaking large datasets into multiple archives limits the impact of any single corrupted archive. For more detail, see the FASRC guide on [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/).
✅ Expected Answer
Tar the directory into subset files first. FASRC recommends that your tar files range in size from 1-100 GiB — that size range transfers more quickly (especially if the transfer is interrupted) and is smaller to retrieve if it ever needs to come back from Tape. If you leave hundreds or thousands of files in a single directory, Globus will go into an 'endpoint is too busy' state and your job will timeout, restart, timeout, etc. FASRC also recommends reading their 'Tips for using tar to archive data' page before tarring.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 47: question_47

❓ Question

I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most common reason for mismatched checksums after `tar`/`untar` is that **the checksum was generated on the original directory, but `tar` doesn't preserve all filesystem metadata** (like permissions, timestamps, ownership, or extended attributes) unless you explicitly tell it to. When you extract the archive, some of these attributes may differ, and depending on how you're computing the checksum, this can cause mismatches. Here are the key things to check: 1. **Use `tar` flags to preserve metadata.** When creating the archive, use: ``` tar -p --numeric-owner --xattrs -cf archive.tar directory/ ``` - `-p` preserves file permissions - `--numeric-owner` preserves ownership numerically - `--xattrs` preserves extended attributes 2. **Checksum before and after using the same method.** Make sure you're comparing checksums of the same files in the same way. The FASRC documentation recommends: ``` find myscans/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > checksums.shasum ``` This generates checksums for all files in the directory. When you extract and verify, run the same command on the extracted directory. 3. **Don't checksum an active filesystem.** If the data is modified between when you generate the checksum and when you untar, the checksums won't match. As noted in the FASRC guide: *"If the data in the directory is modified after you've run the checksum and before you've tar'd it, then the checksum will no longer match later when un-tar and compare later."* [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) 4. **Consider checksumming the tar file itself.** If you want to verify the archive wasn't corrupted during transfer, checksum the `.tar` file directly rather than the extracted contents. Would you like help with a specific command or verifying your checksums?
✅ Expected Answer
The checksum depends on the path you hand to find, so the same files hashed two different ways produce two different values. FASRC's guidance is to always do it the exact same way: cd to the directory *above* the one you are archiving (e.g. /n/mypath/scans) and then run find against the relative directory name (myscans) — not the full path. Two other things will break the comparison: (1) modifying the data after you took the checksum but before you tarred it (a checksum is useless on an active filesystem), and (2) storing the checksum file itself inside the tar, since its presence changes the checksum unless you remove it after un-tarring and before re-running.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving
