Question 48: question_48

❓ Question

RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, your archive is not corrupt. This is expected behavior when extracting a tar file created by Starfish. When Starfish creates a tar archive, it captures the **entire absolute path** of the files inside the archive. So if you archived `/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX`, the archive contains that full path. When you extract it into `/n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/`, it recreates that full path relative to your extraction directory, resulting in: ``` /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX ``` This is why you see a nested tree of empty directories with `ProjectX` buried at the bottom. **To avoid this**, you can extract the archive into a specific directory using the `-C` flag: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar -C /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/ ``` Or, if you want to strip the leading path components, you can use `--strip-components`: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar --strip-components=3 -C /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/ ``` (Adjust the number of components based on your path structure.) For more details, see [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving).
✅ Expected Answer
The archive is fine. Starfish tars in place and captures the entire path inside the tar file, so extracting recreates that whole path underneath your output directory. Extracting a Starfish tar of /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX into /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp gives you /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX. Move the ProjectX directory to where you actually want it, then remove the leftover empty n/holylabs/jharvard_lab tree. Use plain `rm` rather than `rm -rf`, so it errors out if other files remain or you typed the wrong path.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Question 49: question_49

❓ Question

I already pushed a bunch of archives into our lab's tape area on Globus and then realized I named them badly. Can I just rename them, or move a couple into a different folder to tidy things up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, the documentation doesn't explicitly cover renaming or moving files **after** they've been migrated to tape. Here's what I can tell you based on the available sources: ## What you can do 1. **Rename or move files via Globus File Manager** — Since you have write access to the tape collection's disk-based staging/buffer area via Globus, you should be able to rename or move files through the Globus web interface before the data is migrated to tape. The staging area is the disk-based buffer that you write to, and migration to tape happens afterward [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). 2. **If files are already on tape** — The documentation doesn't specify whether rename/move operations work on tape-stored data. Tape systems typically treat files as immutable once archived, so you may need to: - Retrieve the files back to disk first (if retrieval is supported via Globus) - Rename/move them on disk - Re-upload them to the staging area ## Important notes - **Permissions**: Read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus — there's no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within the tape collection [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). - **No FASRC intervention**: The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so we cannot set permissions or intervene directly. Everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). ## Recommendation Try renaming or moving the files through the Globus File Manager first. If that doesn't work (e.g., the files are already on tape and immutable), you may need to retrieve them to disk, reorganize, and re-upload. If you run into issues, reach out to **rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu** for assistance.
✅ Expected Answer
No — do not delete, move, or rename data once it has been transferred to the tape buffer. The Globus buffer gives you write access to upload data destined for tape, and all changes within your lab's Globus collection are automatically picked up by the tape robot. Deletions on the Globus buffer may result in permanent deletion on tape, and deleting files that have already been placed on tape removes the 'link' and makes retrieval impossible. Mass deletions, moves, and file renaming require cumbersome manual intervention. Name files appropriately before you transfer them, and work with your PI or lab data manager on your lab's tape storage protocols.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Args: No query found.

Question 50: question_50

❓ Question

Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's how to set up restricted Globus access for a postdoc to only one project folder: ## The Challenge Globus shares the **entire `Lab` directory** (or `Users/$USER`) — it doesn't natively support sharing only a subfolder. As noted in the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) documentation: > Globus can only see the folders (or directories) `Lab` and `Users/$USER` — as of 2025, the `Users` folder has been deprecated. When using Globus, any folder must be inside `Lab` or `Users/$USER`. ## Recommended Workaround 1. **Create a parent directory** inside the lab share that contains only the project folder(s) you want the postdoc to access. 2. **Copy or move** the relevant project data into this new parent directory. 3. **Share only that parent directory** via Globus with the postdoc. From the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) docs: > "To share files in various directories, you should create a parent directory and copy the various directories to the new parent directory." ## Steps 1. **As the PI or lab admin**, create a new directory in the lab share (e.g., `/n/your_lab/Lab/Postdoc_Project/`). 2. **Copy or move** the specific project data into this directory. 3. **Share this directory** with the postdoc via Globus File Manager — they'll only see this shared folder, not the rest of the lab collection. ## Important Notes - Globus uses your FASRC account's POSIX permissions, so ensure the postdoc has read/write access to this specific directory via standard Unix permissions (`chmod`/`chown`/`setfacl`). - Symlinks do **not** work in Globus, so copying is necessary rather than linking. - Be careful not to share more than necessary — only grant access to the specific project folder. If you need help with the actual Globus sharing steps or permission commands, let me know!
✅ Expected Answer
You cannot. There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus. The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC cannot set those permissions; everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system. (The PI and General Manager can add lab members to the collection, but only to all of it.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access
💬 Agent Messages (5)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Globus share specific folder subdirectory permissions lab collection
🛠️ Tool Call #3: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🛠️ Tool Call #4: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Globus share specific subfolder only not entire lab collection
🤖 Assistant Message #5
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's how to set up restricted Globus access for a postdoc to only one project folder: ## The Challenge Globus shares the **entire `Lab` directory** (or `Users/$USER`) — it doesn't natively support sharing only a subfolder. As noted in the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) documentation: > Globus can only see the folders (or directories) `Lab` and `Users/$USER` — as of 2025, the `Users` folder has been deprecated. When using Globus, any folder must be inside `Lab` or `Users/$USER`. ## Recommended Workaround 1. **Create a parent directory** inside the lab share that contains only the project folder(s) you want the postdoc to access. 2. **Copy or move** the relevant project data into this new parent directory. 3. **Share only that parent directory** via Globus with the postdoc. From the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) docs: > "To share files in various directories, you should create a parent directory and copy the various directories to the new parent directory." ## Steps 1. **As the PI or lab admin**, create a new directory in the lab share (e.g., `/n/your_lab/Lab/Postdoc_Project/`). 2. **Copy or move** the specific project data into this directory. 3. **Share this directory** with the postdoc via Globus File Manager — they'll only see this shared folder, not the rest of the lab collection. ## Important Notes - Globus uses your FASRC account's POSIX permissions, so ensure the postdoc has read/write access to this specific directory via standard Unix permissions (`chmod`/`chown`/`setfacl`). - Symlinks do **not** work in Globus, so copying is necessary rather than linking. - Be careful not to share more than necessary — only grant access to the specific project folder. If you need help with the actual Globus sharing steps or permission commands, let me know!

Question 51: question_51

❓ Question

On Cannon I just do salloc to grab a shell on a compute node, but on FASSE it doesn't work. How am I supposed to get an interactive session there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/matlab, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/Recipe3/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, here's the key difference: **On FASSE, you should use `srun` instead of `salloc` for interactive sessions.** The FASRC documentation explicitly notes: *"do not use salloc on FASSE"* for interactive sessions ([Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/)). Instead, use `srun` to start an interactive session: ```bash srun -p -t --mem --pty /bin/bash ``` For example: ```bash srun -p test -t 10 --mem 1G --pty /bin/bash ``` This allocates resources on a compute node and drops you into an interactive shell there, similar to what `salloc` does on Cannon. The reason for this difference is that FASSE is a secure environment (DSL Level 3) with different security controls and access restrictions, which affects how interactive sessions are managed.
✅ Expected Answer
salloc is deliberately unavailable on FASSE — it is disabled for security reasons, and FASSE does not allow interactive jobs from the command line at all. For interactive work you use the FASSE Open OnDemand (OOD) / VDI virtual desktop at https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, reachable in your browser while connected to the FASRC VPN on the @fasse realm (note @fasse, not the @fasrc realm you use for Cannon). OOD gives you pre-built apps and interactive command-line access in a desktop-like environment. SSH to fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu remains available for batch work.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

How long can a job run on the FASSE H200 GPU nodes, and how many GPUs does each of those nodes have?

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here are the details for the FASSE H200 GPU nodes: * **GPUs per node:** Each node is equipped with **4 Nvidia H200 GPUs** [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). * **Time limit:** Jobs on these nodes (in the `gpu_h200` partition) have a maximum run time of **3 days** [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). To request GPUs, you must include `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:n` in your SLURM submission script, where `n` is between 1 and 4.
✅ Expected Answer
The fasse_gpu_h200 partition has a 3-day time limit — shorter than the 7 days allowed on the other FASSE partitions. There are 2 such nodes, each with 4 H200 GPUs, 112 Intel 'Sapphire Rapids' cores, 990 GB of memory per node, and 843 GB of local /scratch.

Question 53: question_53

❓ Question

A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pi-responsibilities-at-fas-rc

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you download data from a FASSE compute node, the external server will see the IP address of Harvard’s proxy server (**rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu**), not the compute node’s private IP. All outbound internet traffic from FASSE goes through this proxy, so any IP allowlisting you provide to a data provider should be the **public IP address(es) of `rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu`**. You can look up the current public IP(s) for that hostname using a tool like `nslookup` or by checking with FASRC support, since DNS records can change. If you need to verify which IP is being used, you can run a command like: ```bash curl ifconfig.me ``` from a FASSE node (with the proxy set) to see the public IP that external services will observe. Let me know if you'd like help finding the current IP address or configuring the proxy for your specific download tool.
✅ Expected Answer
140.247.232.228. All traffic to the Internet from FASSE — VPN, login, compute, or fasseood — goes out through the FASRC proxy (rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu), which currently has that external IP address. It is subject to change, so you can confirm it by running `curl icanhazip.com` from the node you will actually be working on.
🎯 Expected Source Documents

Question 54: question_54

❓ Question

Our data use agreement says the data has to be encrypted at rest. I was planning to stage the intermediate files for my FASSE jobs in netscratch since it's fast. Is that OK?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-information-on-storage-and-use-of-confidential-data-hrci, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-levels, slurm.schedmd.com/rest.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/HDF5/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what I can tell you: **Netscratch is not rated for sensitive data.** According to the [Data Security Levels](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-levels/) documentation: - **Cannon cluster** (which includes netscratch) is rated only for **DSL1 (Public)** and **DSL2 (Low Risk)** data. - Only the **FASSE cluster** is rated for **DSL3 (Medium Risk)** data. - FASRC has no systems rated for DSL4 (High Risk) or DSL5 (Extreme Risk) data. If your data use agreement requires encryption at rest, you'll need to verify whether your data falls into DSL1/DSL2 or a higher level. Netscratch is a VAST filesystem optimized for performance, but the documentation doesn't indicate it provides encryption at rest — it's designed for high-speed intermediate staging, not for holding sensitive or encrypted data. **What to do next:** 1. **Check your DUA and data classification** — confirm what Data Security Level (DSL) your data falls under. 2. **If your data is DSL3 or higher**, you must use the FASSE cluster, not netscratch. 3. **If your data is DSL1/DSL2** but your DUA specifically requires encryption at rest, you may need to encrypt the data yourself before staging it, or use a different storage solution that supports encryption at rest. 4. **Contact FASRC** — reach out to FASRC support or your project's PI to confirm whether netscratch meets your DUA's encryption-at-rest requirement. The PI is responsible for ensuring compliance with any DUA, so it's worth confirming with FASRC before staging sensitive data on netscratch.
✅ Expected Answer
No. If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data the DUA applies to — neither local scratch nor FASRC's global scratch supports encryption at rest. Separately, DSL 3 / FASSE data should not be stored in your home directory either. You are responsible for knowing and complying with the applicable Harvard Information Security Policy, the Harvard Research Data Security Policy, and any applicable contracts / data use agreements; if you are unsure how this applies to your data, consult FASRC at security@rc.fas.harvard.edu or your School Security Officer.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Question 55: question_55

❓ Question

Our level 3 project storage doesn't show up as a collection in Globus at all. What do we have to do to get it added?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Globus/transfer_globus.py, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, **Level 3 (data security level 3) storage shares are not automatically added to Globus** [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). To get your Level 3 project storage added to Globus, you need to **contact FASRC support** to have it manually added. The documentation explicitly states: > "If you find that your lab share is not seen by Globus or these sub directories don't exist in your lab's share, in the Holyoke or Boston collections, please contact us." When you contact them, specify: - Whether your storage is in the **Holyoke** or **Boston** data center - That it is a **Level 3 (data security level 3)** share FASRC maintains four Globus collections for different combinations of location and security level: - **"Harvard FAS RC Holyoke"** — for lab shares in Holyoke - **"Harvard FAS RC Holyoke Secure"** — for Level 3 shares in Holyoke - **"Harvard FAS RC Boston"** — for lab shares in Boston - **"Harvard FAS RC Boston Secure"** — for Level 3 shares in Boston Once added, you'll access your Level 3 data through the appropriate "Secure" collection. Note that Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` and `Users/$USER` — directories like `Everyone` and `Transfer` are not available through Globus.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is broken: DSL 3 / FASSE storage is intentionally not included in Globus by default. To have it exposed, first review the governing DUA — Globus makes data available over the Internet without a VPN or private network, so a DUA requiring VPN/private-network access for all access to the data would not be Globus-compliant (consult your School Security Officer if unsure). Then submit a ticket to rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu that (1) includes the path to the project to add (e.g. /n/piname_project_l3), (2) states that the PI attests Globus is compliant with any contracts/DUAs governing the data, and (3) is either sent by the PI or receives a direct reply from the PI confirming it.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Question 56: question_56

❓ Question

How much CPU and RAM am I actually allowed on the FASRC login server, and is there a cap on how many terminal windows I can have open at once?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cgroup.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-from-abroad, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation: **CPU and RAM limits on login nodes:** The Rocky 8 login nodes use a system-level mechanism called **cgroups** which limits each logged-in account to **1 CPU core and 4GB of memory** ([Rocky 8 Transition Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide/)). **Terminal windows/sessions:** The documentation doesn't specify a hard cap on the number of terminal windows or SSH sessions you can have open at once. However, each session is subject to the same 1-core/4GB memory limit per account. **Important note:** Login nodes are meant as gateways for launching and monitoring jobs and running light processes to prepare your jobs or environment. If you need more CPU or memory, you should use an interactive job via `salloc` instead ([Rocky 8 Transition Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide/)).
✅ Expected Answer
Each login-node session is limited to 1 core and 8GB of memory, and you may have at most 5 sessions per user. Users who abuse the login nodes may have their sessions terminated, and the login nodes are rebooted during monthly maintenance to clear out stale sessions. If you need more than 5 sessions, switch to batch jobs, use Open OnDemand (which gives you a desktop with multiple terminals on a dedicated compute node), or use screen/tmux to multiplex one session.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Question 57: question_57

❓ Question

I ran ssh-copy-id and my key works great when I'm in the office, but from my apartment it still makes me punch in the 6-digit code every single time. Did the key not install properly?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-key-error, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/synchronizing-clocks

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your SSH key likely installed correctly — the fact that it works without a 6-digit code from the office suggests the key authentication is functioning as intended. The 6-digit code you're being prompted for is almost certainly **Harvard's multi-factor authentication (MFA)**, not an SSH key issue. Here's what's happening: - **On-campus (office):** When you're connected to the Harvard network (via wired or campus Wi-Fi), your connection may be trusted or routed through internal systems that don't require the MFA step for SSH access. - **Off-campus (apartment):** When you're outside the Harvard network, SSH connections to the cluster typically require MFA (the 6-digit code) as an additional security layer, regardless of whether your SSH key is valid. This is standard security practice — SSH keys authenticate *who you are*, but MFA adds a second factor when connecting from untrusted networks. **To resolve this from home, you'll likely need to:** 1. **Connect via Harvard VPN** first, then SSH — this routes your traffic through the trusted internal network and may bypass the MFA prompt. 2. **Use the MFA code** each time you SSH from off-campus, which is the expected behavior. If you'd like more details on setting up the VPN or configuring SSH for off-campus access, I can help with that.
✅ Expected Answer
The key is fine — this is by design. Passwordless (ssh-key) authentication is only permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks, including Harvard/FASRC VPNs. If you connect from an external network you are still required to provide two-factor authentication regardless of your key. So either connect to the VPN from home, or accept the 2FA prompt. (On FASSE, passwordless ssh is permitted from the FASSE VPN only.) You can confirm the key installed by checking that it is present in ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the cluster.

❓ Question

I'm an IQSS researcher and my survey data is classified Level 2. Which cluster am I supposed to be on, and what exactly goes in the VPN box versus the cluster login box?

🎯 Retrieval Check

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Good news — for **Level 2 (DSL2)** data, you should be on the **Cannon cluster**, not FASSE. ✅ Expected Answer
Level 2 data belongs on Cannon, the FAS research computing cluster for Data Security Level 2 (only Level 3 data must move to the FASSE secure environment). To get on: in the Cisco AnyConnect VPN client use server address vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu and enter your FASRC username in the format username@fasrc, your FASRC password, and your FASRC 2FA token — note this is NOT the code associated with your HarvardKey account. Then log into the Cannon cluster at https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu using just your FASRC username WITHOUT the '@fasrc', plus your FASRC password.

Question 59: question_59

❓ Question

My pandas script keeps blowing up with a memory error because the file is bigger than the node's RAM. Does FASRC want me to rewrite it, or can I keep using pandas somehow?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/Example2/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You don't need to rewrite your script from scratch — you can keep using pandas, but you should process the file in **chunks** so it never exceeds the node's RAM. ### Quick fix: use `chunksize` Instead of loading the entire file at once: ```python import pandas as pd chunk_iter = pd.read_csv("big_file.csv", chunksize=100_000) # adjust as needed for chunk in chunk_iter: # process each chunk (e.g., filter, aggregate, transform) result = process(chunk) # accumulate or write results incrementally ``` This way, only one chunk lives in memory at a time. ### Tips for chunked pandas on FASRC - **Tune `chunksize`** so each chunk fits comfortably in your node's memory (leave headroom for other processes). - **Write intermediate results** to disk (e.g., CSV, Parquet, or HDF5) instead of accumulating everything in memory. - **Request enough memory** in your SLURM job: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=16G # or higher, depending on your chunk size ``` - If you're doing heavy numerical work, consider **NumPy** or **Dask** for out-of-core or parallel processing. ### When to consider alternatives If your analysis requires the **entire dataset in memory** (e.g., full joins, complex groupbys), you'll need to either: 1. **Request a larger node** (e.g., `--mem=64G` or more, or use a high-memory partition if available). 2. **Switch to Dask** or **Spark**, which handle out-of-core computation natively. Would you like help estimating a good `chunksize` for your file, or guidance on setting up a Dask job on FASRC?
✅ Expected Answer
Both options exist, and which one FASRC recommends depends on scale. Start with chunking. For moderately large datasets, Dask can enhance Pandas by parallelizing operations — you can convert a Pandas DataFrame to a Dask DataFrame with dd.from_pandas() and keep most of your existing code. But for very large datasets that exceed memory constraints, using Dask alone as a substitute for Pandas is the more effective solution: Dask is a drop-in replacement with a familiar DataFrame API that works lazily, breaking work into smaller tasks that only execute when you call .compute(). Separately, switching from CSV to a columnar format like Parquet (e.g. df.to_parquet('output_data.parquet', compression='snappy')) greatly improves I/O performance.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 60: question_60

❓ Question

I switched to the newest Intel module on the cluster and now the compiler command I've always used doesn't exist anymore. What am I supposed to call to build my C file now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/Armadillo/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, when you switch to the newest Intel module (like `intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`), the compiler command has changed from the older `icc` to **`icx`** (Intel oneAPI C compiler). The new Intel oneAPI setup provides: - **`icx`** - for C compilation - **`icpx`** - for C++ compilation So instead of your old command like: ```bash icc my_file.c -o my_program ``` You should now use: ```bash icx my_file.c -o my_program ``` This change reflects Intel's transition to the oneAPI programming model. The older `icc` command may still be available in some module versions, but the recommended command for the newest Intel modules is `icx`. Would you like help with any specific compilation flags or options for the new compiler?
✅ Expected Answer
Intel renamed the compiler binaries at version 24.0.0. For Intel modules below 24.0.0 the commands are icc (C), ifortran (Fortran), and icpc (C++). For Intel 24.0.0 and above they are icx (C), ifx (Fortran), and icpx (C++). So with a newer Intel module you must invoke icx instead of icc. (You can list the available Intel compilers with `module spider intel` and load a specific one, e.g. `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid

Question 61: question_61

❓ Question

Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, that's not recommended. According to the FASRC documentation: 1. **Don't compile on the login node** — You should request an interactive job on a compute node (e.g., via `salloc`) to compile your C program, since compilation can take up to a minute depending on complexity [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). 2. **Don't put the `gcc` line in your `sbatch` script** — It's best practice to compile your code separately, then use the generated executable in your production batch job. Including the compilation command in the `sbatch` script will recompile the program every time you submit the job [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). **Recommended workflow:** - Compile separately on a compute node (e.g., `salloc` into the `test` partition to compile and test) - Once you have a working executable, submit your production run via `sbatch` without the compilation step - If you change the source code, recompile separately, then submit the batch job again
✅ Expected Answer
No on both counts. FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile a C program (compilation can take a few seconds to a minute depending on the code's complexity), and specifically recommends using the `test` partition to compile and test the program before running the production job. Best practice is to compile the code separately and then use the resulting executable in your sbatch script — avoid putting the compilation command inside the sbatch script, because that recompiles the program on every submission. If you change the source, recompile separately and then resubmit the production batch job.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 62: question_62

❓ Question

I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/Notebook.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
There isn't a single "uninstall" command for a user-level Julia installation on the cluster. When you install Julia locally (e.g., via `curl -fsSL https://install.julialang.org | sh`), it: 1. **Downloads Julia to `$HOME`** (typically into a directory like `~/.juliaup/` or `~/.julia/`) 2. **Adds `juliaup` initialization to `~/.bashrc`** so `julia` is on your `$PATH` To start clean, you need to do **both** of these steps: ### 1. Remove the Julia installation directory The installer places files in `~/.juliaup/` (and possibly `~/.julia/` for packages). Remove them: ```bash rm -rf ~/.juliaup ~/.julia ``` ### 2. Remove the `juliaup` entry from `~/.bashrc` Edit `~/.bashrc` and delete the lines that `juliaup` added (they typically look like `eval "$($HOME/.juliaup/bin/juliaup shell-mode ...)"` or similar). You can search for it: ```bash grep -n "juliaup" ~/.bashrc ``` Then remove those lines. ### Does it leave junk behind? Yes, if you only run one command: - **If you only delete the Julia directory**, your `~/.bashrc` still has the `juliaup` initialization, which will error on login. - **If you only clean `~/.bashrc`**, the Julia binaries and packages remain in your home directory, wasting space. So you need **both** steps above for a truly clean removal. For more details, see the [Julia Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Run `juliaup self uninstall`. That is the uninstall command, but it does not always clean everything up: afterward, check for the `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup` directories and delete them manually if they are still there. (This matters because the default Julia install location is $HOME, and the installer also adds Juliaup initialization to your ~/.bashrc to put `julia` on your $PATH.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Question 63: question_63

❓ Question

I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unix-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue is almost certainly **directory permissions on the parent share or intermediate directories**, not the file itself. Here's why: ## The "Other" permission chain When you `chmod 777` a file, you're granting read/write/execute to **Other** (everyone). But for another user to reach that file, they must also have **execute (traverse) permission on every parent directory** leading to it. If any directory in the path has permissions like `drwxr-x---` (no "Other" access), the collaborator will be blocked at that directory level, regardless of the file's permissions. As the FASRC documentation explains: > "Note that for `Other` to actually have access, they would need also to be able to access the top-level share itself... If the top-level of the lab directory or share is set `drwxr-x---` or `drwxrwx`— or `drwxr-s---`, then `Other` has **no** access to the directory/share, even if a file inside is set `-rwxrwxrwx`." [Understanding file and group permissions](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions/) ## What to check Run this to see the full permission chain: ```bash ls -ld /path/to/share /path/to/share/* ``` Look at the permissions on the **share root** and any intermediate directories. If they show `---` for "Other" (the last three characters), that's your blocker. ## Better alternatives Instead of `chmod 777`, consider: 1. **Add the collaborator to your group** (if appropriate) — they'll get group-level access. 2. **Use Globus** to share files with external collaborators without needing FASRC accounts [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer). 3. **Use `setfacl`** to grant specific users access without opening permissions to everyone. Note: FASSE/L3 shares should **never** allow `Other` access, so `chmod 777` is not appropriate there.
✅ Expected Answer
Making the file world-readable is not enough on its own. For a user in the 'Other' category to reach a file, they must first be able to traverse into the top-level share/directory that contains it. If the top level of the lab directory or share is set drwxr-x---, drwxrwx---, or drwxr-s--- then Other has no access to the share at all, even if a file inside it is set -rwxrwxrwx. Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share; otherwise it has no access. (FASSE/L3 shares should never allow Other access.) To share with someone outside the group, the enclosing directory must permit Other traversal - e.g. the 'Everyone' directory that lab shares provide for exactly this purpose - or the person can be added to your lab Group by your PI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 64: question_64

❓ Question

My labmate keeps his personal scratch folder under Users/ in our old lab share, but our newly created share and netscratch have no Users folder. Where am I supposed to put my own personal directory now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know about where to put your personal files: ### On your new Lab Storage (e.g., `/n/holylabs//`) The new lab share structure has two top-level directories: **`Lab/`** and **`Everyone/`** [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). - **`Lab/`** – This is your primary working directory and is shared with the whole lab. Individual users can create their own subdirectories here (e.g., `/n/holylabs//Lab/your_username/`). You can adjust permissions on your own folders, though FASRC **highly recommends** keeping access open to all lab members for easier collaboration and data cleanup [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). - **`Everyone/`** – This directory is visible to anyone on the cluster and is intended for cross-lab collaboration. It is not available via Globus. So instead of the old `Users/` folder, you should create your personal directory directly under **`Lab/`**, like: ``` /n/holylabs//Lab/your_username/ ``` ### On netscratch (`/n/netscratch/`) Netscratch is **temporary, high-performance scratch space** with a 90-day retention policy and no backups [Scratch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/). It is not intended for personal file storage. Files older than 90 days will be purged, and the purge can run at any time, especially if scratch is getting full. You should use netscratch only for transient job data, not for personal files you want to keep. ### Summary - **Personal files** → Create a subdirectory under your lab's **`Lab/`** folder. - **Temporary job data** → Use **`/n/netscratch/`** (but don't rely on it for long-term storage). - The old `Users/` structure is from the legacy storage system and is no longer used in the new lab shares.
✅ Expected Answer
The Users directory only exists on shares created prior to October 2024. On new shares and on /n/netscratch there is no Users directory - FASRC says to use the Lab directory to create personal folders instead. The Lab directory is set drwxrws--- so that every member of the lab group has read/write/execute access to share data amongst the group, and it is the preferred place both for personal folders on new shares and for sharing data with your lab group. On the older shares, the sub-directories in the Users directory are owned by individuals and by default only allow execute access to the group.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 65: question_65

❓ Question

I finished the cluster account request form and it never asked me to choose a password. How do I actually get a password to log in?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cant-login-cluster-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/reset-password, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/get-a-fasse-account-and-project-group, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You don't choose a password during the account request form. Instead, you set it afterward by using the password reset portal: 1. Go to **[https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/p3/pwreset/](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/p3/pwreset/)** 2. Enter the email address you used to sign up and click **Submit** 3. Check your email for a one-time link and click it 4. Enter a new password on the form that appears **Password requirements:** * At least 12 characters * Both uppercase and lowercase letters * A number and a special character (!@#$%^&*) * Must not contain a portion of your name or username Once you've set your password, you can log in to the cluster using your FASRC username and that password. For more details, see the [Reset Password](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/reset-password/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Passwords are not collected by the signup application. Your account is created with a random password, and upon completion of the request you use the password reset tool to set your initial password. You will receive an email once the account has been created with links and instructions to do so.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here are the steps you can try yourself before contacting support: 1. **Try a different browser.** The documentation specifically notes that **Firefox works best** for FASRC web tools, while Chrome and other browsers sometimes cause errors [Request an FAS Research Computing Account](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/). 2. **Clear your browser cache and cookies.** A spinning portal after login is often caused by stale session data or corrupted cache. Clearing these and restarting the browser can resolve the issue. 3. **Try a private/incognito window.** This will load the portal without any extensions or cached data, helping you determine if an extension is interfering. 4. **Check your VPN connection.** If you are trying to access certain portal features (like the Open OnDemand dashboard), you may need to be connected to the FASRC VPN [Virtual Desktop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop/). If these steps don't resolve the issue, you can submit a ticket via the [FASRC Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/rcrt/tickets/).
✅ Expected Answer
If the browser hangs for a long time after authenticating without displaying the Open OnDemand dashboard, restart your Per-User NGINX (PUN) process by visiting: - Cannon: https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ - FASSE: https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ Also note that the very first login can legitimately take some time while OOD sets up files and cache in your home directory; that Avast security software can cause 'no internet', 'read error', and 'the requested URL could not be retrieved' messages (disable Web Shield); and that Chrome or Firefox are recommended over Safari and Internet Explorer.

Question 67: question_67

❓ Question

My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/EP/Example1/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/OpenMP/Example2/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/OpenACC/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The short answer: **don't hard-code a worker count**. Instead, read the number of CPUs Slurm allocated to your job from an environment variable and use that as your worker count. ### The key environment variable When you request CPUs in your SLURM script (e.g., `#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8`), SLURM sets the variable **`SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`** to that value. You can read it inside your R script: ```r n_workers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) ``` If you didn't request `--cpus-per-task` but instead requested `--ntasks`, you can use **`SLURM_NTASKS`** instead. In many R parallel setups, `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` is the right choice because it represents the total CPU cores available to your job step. ### Why this fixes the problem If your R script hard-codes a worker count (e.g., `parallelly::availableCores()` or a fixed `makeCluster(32)`), it may exceed the CPUs Slurm actually allocated, causing oversubscription and the "node grinds to a halt" behavior you described. By deriving the worker count from `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`, your parallel code stays within the resources you asked for. ### Example SLURM + R setup ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=my_r_job #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8 #SBATCH --mem=16G Rscript my_parallel_script.R ``` ```r # Inside my_parallel_script.R n_workers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) # Use n_workers to configure your parallel backend ``` ### If you also use OpenMP or other thread-based parallelism Be aware that some R packages (or libraries they call) may spawn their own threads. If you're using packages like `data.table` or `Rcpp` with OpenMP, set `OMP_NUM_THREADS` to avoid double-scheduling: ```bash export OMP_NUM_THREADS=$SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK ``` This ensures that the total thread count across all parallel backends doesn't exceed your allocation. ### Sources - SLURM sets `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` to the count of CPUs allocated per task, which you can read in your job script or application [Slurm srun docs](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html). - Example code from FASRC training materials demonstrates reading `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` from the environment and using it to cap worker counts [Distributed GPU Computing Exercise 5 README](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md).
✅ Expected Answer
Do not use parallel::detectCores() to determine the number of CPUs/processes to use in your job - it detects the number of physical CPU cores on the compute node, not the number allocated to your job, which is why you end up oversubscribed. Use parallelly::availableCores() instead, which returns the number of CPUs allocated to your Slurm job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Question 68: question_68

❓ Question

I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example4/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example2/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example3/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example5/build.sh

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you haven't corrupted your environment. This is expected behavior. MPI implementations on the FASRC cluster are mutually exclusive. When you load a second MPI module (like `mpich`) after already having one loaded (like `openmpi`), the module system replaces the first MPI library with the second. This is by design, as mixing MPI implementations can cause conflicts and undefined behavior in compiled applications. **Can you keep both around?** You can have both modules available in your system, but you can only have **one MPI implementation loaded at a time** in a given session. To switch back to OpenMPI, simply run: ```bash module unload mpich module load openmpi/ ``` Or, if you want to start fresh: ```bash module purge module load openmpi/ ``` For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface).
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is corrupted - this is intentional. The modules are set up so that you can only have one MPI module loaded at a time; if you try loading a second one it will automatically unload the first, which is done to avoid dependency collisions. So you cannot keep both loaded: pick one implementation (OpenMPI or MPICH, each built with either the GNU or the Intel compiler suite) and load it with its matching compiler module, e.g. module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01. Note also that there is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in - you must load one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
No, you haven't corrupted your environment. This is expected behavior. MPI implementations on the FASRC cluster are mutually exclusive. When you load a second MPI module (like `mpich`) after already having one loaded (like `openmpi`), the module system replaces the first MPI library with the second. This is by design, as mixing MPI implementations can cause conflicts and undefined behavior in compiled applications. **Can you keep both around?** You can have both modules available in your system, but you can only have **one MPI implementation loaded at a time** in a given session. To switch back to OpenMPI, simply run: ```bash module unload mpich module load openmpi/<version> ``` Or, if you want to start fresh: ```bash module purge module load openmpi/<version> ``` For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface).

Question 69: question_69

❓ Question

I need mpi4py in a conda environment, but it has to be built against the same OpenMPI my Fortran code uses. I just ran mamba create with mpi4py in the package list - what MPI did that actually give me, and how do I get OpenMPI instead?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/Example1/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you ran `mamba create ... mpi4py`, it installed **MPICH** by default, not OpenMPI. The FASRC documentation notes that the straightforward `mamba create` approach "will install a MPI library required by mpi4py. By default, the above commands will install MPICH" [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). To get **mpi4py built against OpenMPI** (matching your Fortran code), you need to: 1. **Load the OpenMPI module first** (before creating/installing): ```bash module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01 # or your preferred compiler module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03 # or the OpenMPI version your Fortran code uses ``` 2. **Create a conda environment without mpi4py in the initial command**: ```bash mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel source activate python3_env2 ``` 3. **Install mpi4py via pip** (so it picks up the loaded OpenMPI): ```bash pip install mpi4py ``` This approach ensures mpi4py links against the OpenMPI libraries provided by the loaded module, keeping it consistent with your Fortran code's MPI backend.
✅ Expected Answer
Putting mpi4py in the mamba create package list (e.g. mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py) also installs an MPI library required by mpi4py, and by default that is MPICH - not OpenMPI. If your workflow requires a specific flavor and/or version of MPI, install mpi4py with pip into a custom conda environment instead: load the compiler and MPI modules first (module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01; module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03), load a Python module (module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01), create the environment WITHOUT mpi4py (mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel), activate it (source activate python3_env2), then run pip install mpi4py so it builds against the loaded OpenMPI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 70: question_70

❓ Question

I used the 'copy mount path to clipboard' button in Starfish and got /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab, but when I cd to it on a login node I get 'No such file or directory'. What's the correct path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The path you copied from Starfish is the **network mount path**, which is only accessible when the storage is mounted on your local machine (via Samba/SMB). It is **not** the path you use on FASRC login or compute nodes. On FASRC login and compute nodes, the correct path to your lab directory is: ``` /n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` Note the difference: - **Starfish mount path (for local desktop/laptop):** `/n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` - **FASRC cluster path (for login/compute nodes):** `/n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` You can verify this by running: ```bash ls /n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` For more details on FASRC storage paths, see [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
The path Starfish copies is not directly usable - you must make two edits: remove 'vast-' and remove '/C'. So /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab becomes /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab. The letter C indicates the filesystem is on the Cannon cluster; a group share on FASSE would show F instead of C before the group name.

Question 71: question_71

❓ Question

I want to drag a few files out of my home directory into my lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface, but my home directory doesn't show up as an endpoint in Globus. What else can I use?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your home directory isn't available as a Globus endpoint, you can use the **Open OnDemand Remote Desktop** to drag and drop files via a point-and-click file manager. Here's how: 1. Go to the Open OnDemand dashboard: - Cannon: https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard - FASSE: https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard 2. Start a **Remote Desktop** session ([Remote Desktop docs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vdi-apps/#Remote_Desktop)) 3. Once the session starts, open the **File Manager** by clicking the "Home" folder on the desktop (or via **Applications → File Manager**) 4. Open **two** File Manager windows: - Navigate one to your **source** folder (e.g., your home directory) - Navigate the other to your **destination** folder (e.g., your lab's netscratch folder) 5. Drag and drop files between the two windows This gives you a graphical, point-and-click interface for moving files between directories that may not both be Globus endpoints. For more details, see [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Globus does not have access to home directories, which is why yours does not appear. For transfers to or from your home directory, use an Open OnDemand Remote Desktop session instead: go to the OOD dashboard (Cannon: rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, FASSE: fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu), start a Remote Desktop session, open a File Manager by clicking the 'Home' folder on the desktop (or Applications -> File Manager), open a second File Manager window, navigate one to the source (e.g. /n/home01/jharvard/) and the other to the destination (e.g. /n/netscratch/jharvard_lab/Lab/), then right-click the source file -> Copy and right-click the destination window -> Paste (drag-and-drop works too, but behaves like cut-and-paste). Command-line tools (cp, mv, rsync, fpsync) are the alternative; Globus is only recommended for transfers between different filesystems, not within one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, your collaborator can get an account even without a HarvardKey. According to FASRC's [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/), if she is a faculty member at another university collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should choose **EXTERNAL** when requesting an account. Similarly, if she is a student or researcher at another university collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should also choose **EXTERNAL**. As for keeping her access active, the documentation notes that: - All active FAS RC account holders are subscribed to a notifications mailing list, which is a requirement for all users [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). - Cluster access is intended for legitimate purposes benefiting Harvard research and must be authorized by the faculty or management of FAS and Research Computing staff [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). You should ensure she: 1. Agrees to follow Harvard's acceptable use and security policies 2. Remains engaged in the collaborative research project 3. Continues to receive and read the notifications mailing list If you have specific concerns about account expiration or maintenance, I'd recommend checking with FASRC directly or reviewing the [Acceptable Use](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/) guidelines for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Question 73: question_73

❓ Question

I'm a new PI with a HarvardKey but the system won't let me approve my grad student's FASRC account request. What do I need to do?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what's likely happening and what you need to do: ### The Issue FASRC accounts are **separate from HarvardKey** ([Cluster Account Signup](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup/)). Even with a HarvardKey, your PI approval rights depend on your account being properly set up in the FAS RC system with the correct **Job Title** and **affiliation**. ### What You Need to Do 1. **Verify your own RC account status** — Make sure you have an active FAS RC account with the correct job title: - If you're a Harvard faculty member with a supported affiliation, you should have selected **FACULTY** during signup ([Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/)). - If you have PI rights but a non-faculty appointment, you should have selected **NON-FACULTY PI** — these accounts require vetting before approval rights are granted. 2. **Check your lab group setup** — You need to be associated with a lab group in the [FAS RC Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/grants/add) to approve student requests. If you don't see your lab group listed, you may need to add it through the portal. 3. **If your account was just created** — PI approval rights may not be active immediately. For **NON-FACULTY PI** accounts, FASRC staff must vet and approve the account before you can sponsor others. ### Next Steps - Log in to the [FAS RC Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and check your profile to confirm your job title and lab group affiliations. - If you're a **NON-FACULTY PI**, contact FASRC directly — they may need to complete vetting of your account before you can approve student requests. - If you're a **FACULTY** member and still can't approve, reach out to FASRC support to verify your lab group is properly configured. Would you like help with any of these steps?

Globus isn't broken—your dataset just isn't in a Globus-visible location. According to the FASRC data storage documentation, the **Everyone** directory is "not available on Globus and is intended only for internal sharing" [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). Globus only exposes the **Lab** directory by default. To share data with another lab via Globus, you'll need to: 1. Move or copy the dataset into your **Lab** directory instead 2. Ensure the receiving lab has been granted access to that Lab directory (or a subfolder within it) If you need help with moving the data or setting up Globus access, let me know!
✅ Expected Answer
No, that is expected behavior. The Everyone subdirectory is visible to anyone on the HPC cluster and is intended only for internal sharing with other labs on the cluster — it is not available on Globus. The Lab subdirectory is the one shared out via Globus, so data that has to move over Globus needs to live under Lab.

Yes—there is already a pre-installed `samtools` Singularity container on the cluster. You can find it at: ``` /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 ``` You can run it directly without pulling anything yourself, for example: ```bash singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help ``` This image is hosted on CVMFS (the Galaxy Project's Singularity library) and is available to all users without needing to pull from Docker Hub. See the [Singularity documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster/) for more details on using Singularity containers on FASRC clusters.
✅ Expected Answer

Cluster nodes automount a CernVM-File System at /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/, which provides Singularity images for the BioContainers project (auto-generated from Bioconda packages). Images follow the layout /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/FIRST_LETTER/SECOND_LETTER/PACKAGE_NAME:VERSION--CONDA_BUILD, for example: `singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help`. Expect a 10-30 second delay the first time the path is accessed on a compute node that has not mounted/cached it.

Question 77: question_77

❓ Question

A spack install blew up and by the time I looked the build log was already gone. How do I stop spack from throwing the logs away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/augustus-install.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to search the knowledge base for methods to preserve Spack build logs after a failed installation, executing multiple hybrid vector searches for terms like "spack keep build logs" and "prevent deletion." However, the process hit the recursion limit of 50 without finding a specific configuration flag in the retrieved documents, which primarily covered general Spack introduction and compiler setup. To answer your request: Spack automatically deletes build logs for successful installations to save space, but it **keeps logs for failed installations** by default in the `install_tree` directory (specifically under `logs/`). If your logs are gone, it is likely because the installation actually succeeded (perhaps with warnings you missed) or the logs were cleaned up by a post-installation hook. To ensure logs are preserved for debugging in the future, you can: 1. **Check the `install_tree`**: Look in `$(spack root)/opt/spack/linux-.../logs/` for the package name. 2. **Use `--keep-stage`**: Run `spack install --keep-stage `. This prevents Spack from deleting the build directory entirely, allowing you to inspect files directly. 3. **Configure `config.yaml`**: You can add `install_tree: {keep_logs: true}` to your `~/.spack/config.yaml` to force Spack to keep logs for all installations (both successful and failed), though this consumes more disk space. The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
Spack builds in a `stage` directory located in /tmp and cleans that space up once it finishes building, whether the build succeeded or failed — which is why the logs disappear. To keep them, first set the `$TMP` environment variable to a location you want the stage files dumped into, then add the `--keep-stage` flag, e.g. `spack install --keep-stage `, which tells spack to keep the staging files rather than remove them.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

However, the process hit the recursion limit of 50 without finding a specific configuration flag in the retrieved documents, which primarily covered general Spack introduction and compiler setup. To answer your request: Spack automatically deletes build logs for successful installations to save space, but it **keeps logs for failed installations** by default in the `install_tree` directory (specifically under `logs/`). If your logs are gone, it is likely because the installation actually succeeded (perhaps with warnings you missed) or the logs were cleaned up by a post-installation hook. To ensure logs are preserved for debugging in the future, you can: 1. **Check the `install_tree`**: Look in `$(spack root)/opt/spack/linux-.../logs/` for the package name. 2. **Use `--keep-stage`**: Run `spack install --keep-stage <package>`. This prevents Spack from deleting the build directory entirely, allowing you to inspect files directly. 3. **Configure `config.yaml`**: You can add `install_tree: {keep_logs: true}` to your `~/.spack/config.yaml` to force Spack to keep logs for all installations (both successful and failed), though this consumes more disk space. Question 78: question_78

❓ Question

Software I built with spack runs fine on the node I built it on but won't run on nodes in other partitions. How do I build something that works everywhere on the cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/augustus-install.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/software

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to resolve the issue of non-portable Spack binaries by searching the knowledge base for strategies to build software that works across different cluster partitions. The search queries focused on "spack build portable binary," "cluster partitions," and "architecture" compatibility. **Summary of Attempt:** The agent retrieved documentation regarding Spack usage, including environment activation scripts and general introduction to Spack as a package manager for supercomputers. However, the specific retrieved documents focused on basic installation and environment activation rather than the specific compiler flags or configuration settings required for cross-partition portability. **Answer:** The issue you are experiencing is likely due to the software being compiled with optimizations specific to the CPU architecture of the node where you built it (e.g., using `-march=native` or specific AVX instructions). To build software that works everywhere on the cluster, you should: 1. **Use a Generic Target:** When configuring Spack, ensure you are not using architecture-specific optimizations. You can specify a generic target in your `spack.yaml` or via command line flags (e.g., `spack -t generic` or setting `target` to a generic architecture like `x86_64` or `zen` depending on your cluster's support, rather than a specific micro-architecture). 2. **Check Compiler Flags:** Ensure your Spack configuration (`compilers.yaml` or `packages.yaml`) does not pass aggressive optimization flags like `-march=native` which tie the binary to the specific CPU of the build node. 3. **Use a Shared Build Node:** If possible, build your software on a generic compute node that is representative of the cluster's baseline architecture, or use a dedicated build environment that enforces portable compilation flags. 4. **Verify Dependencies:** Ensure that all dependencies are also built with portable flags, as a single non-portable dependency can break the entire application on other nodes. The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
By default Spack autodetects the architecture of the hardware it is building on and optimizes the build for it, so a build made on one CPU generation may not run on another (the docs give the example that LAMMPS built on Sapphire Rapids may not run on Cascade Lake). If you plan to run across heterogeneous hardware/partitions, set Spack to a generic architecture: edit `etc/spack/packages.yaml` inside the spack folder (create it if it does not exist) and add: packages: all: target: [x86_64]
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack
