Question 79: question_79

❓ Question

I don't want spack spending hours compiling its own OpenMPI — how do I make it use the openmpi module we already have on the cluster, and is there anything I have to do before installing?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
1) **Summary of Attempt**: The agent attempted to retrieve specific configuration instructions for using Spack with system-installed MPI libraries (specifically OpenMPI) by querying the vector store for documents related to "spack use system openmpi module" and general compiler configuration. 2) **Best Possible Answer**: To make Spack use the OpenMPI module already available on your cluster instead of compiling its own, you need to configure Spack to recognize the system compiler and then explicitly tell Spack to use the system MPI. **Steps to follow:** 1. **Ensure the module is loaded**: Before configuring Spack, load the OpenMPI module in your shell so Spack can detect the paths. ```bash module load openmpi ``` 2. **Configure the Compiler (if not already done)**: Spack needs to know which compiler to use. If you are using a compiler provided by a module (like GCC or Intel), you must add it to Spack's configuration. * Edit `~/.spack/packages.yaml` (or use `spack compiler add` if the compiler is in your PATH). * Ensure the compiler paths match the ones provided by the module. 3. **Install OpenMPI with the `+wrapper` flag or specify the system MPI**: The most direct way to avoid recompiling is to install packages that depend on MPI using the `openmpi` variant from the system, or by configuring Spack to use the system's MPI binaries. However, Spack typically manages its own MPI instances. The recommended approach to "use the system module" is often to **not install OpenMPI via Spack at all**, but rather to ensure your Spack-built packages link against the system MPI. * **Option A (Recommended for simplicity)**: Do not install `openmpi` via Spack. Instead, when installing your target package (e.g., `gromacs`, `lammps`), specify the `openmpi` variant and ensure Spack uses the system compiler. You may need to set the `mpi` provider in `packages.yaml`. * **Option B (Explicit System MPI)**: In `~/.spack/packages.yaml`, you can configure the `openmpi` package to use the system paths, but this is complex. A simpler method is to use the `spack install` command with the `~openmpi` flag if the package supports multiple MPI providers, or rely on the system MPI being in the `PATH`. **Crucial Pre-installation Step**: You must ensure that the `openmpi` module is loaded in the environment where you run `spack install`. Spack will detect the `mpicc`, `mpirun`, etc., from the module if they are in your `PATH`. Example `packages.yaml` snippet to prioritize system compilers: ```yaml compilers: - compiler: spec: gcc@9.3.0 paths: cc: /usr/bin/gcc cxx: /usr/bin/g++ f77: /usr/bin/gfortran fc: /usr/bin/gfortran flags: {} operating_system: rhel8 target: x86_64 modules: [] environment: {} extra_rpaths: [] ``` Then, when installing: ```bash spack install %gcc openmpi=openmpi ``` *Note: If Spack insists on compiling its own OpenMPI, you may need to explicitly disable the `openmpi` dependency in the package spec or use the `system` variant if available.* 3) **Note**: The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
Configure the cluster's OpenMPI as an external package in `~/.spack/packages.yaml` (create the file if it does not exist). Get the prefix by loading the modules and reading $MPI_HOME, then add: packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@5.0.5%gcc@14.2.0 prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/Comp/gcc/14.2.0-fasrc01/openmpi/5.0.5-fasrc01 buildable: false The `buildable: false` option ensures MPI won't be built from source and Spack will use the module in that prefix instead. Before installing, run `module purge` — this is required or the build fails (e.g. `module purge` then `spack install hdf5@1.14.6 % gcc@14.2.0 ^ openmpi@5.0.5`).

❓ Question

We cleaned out several TB of old data last month but this month's storage charge came in exactly the same. Is the bill wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spinal-expense-codes, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The bill is likely correct. According to FASRC's [Data Storage Billing](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing/) documentation: > The monthly charge for RC Storage is based on the storage tier and the **total size of the allocation**, not the amount of used space. As a result, deleting files will not reduce your monthly cost unless the allocation size is also reduced. In other words, you're charged for the total amount of storage you've been allocated (e.g., 10 TiB), regardless of how much of it you actually use. Simply deleting files doesn't lower the charge — you would need to formally reduce your allocation size through the Storage Service Center. If you believe there's an error (e.g., the allocation size itself was changed), you can contact [RC Storage Billing](mailto:billing@rc.fas.harvard.edu) or review the bill in the FIINE system within 3 business days of receiving the notification.
✅ Expected Answer
No, the bill is correct. The monthly charge is based on the size of the storage allocation, not on the amount of storage actually used, so deleting files will not reduce the charge unless the allocation size itself is also reduced. Separately, the cutoff for billing changes is the 15th of each month — any change made to an allocation after the 15th is only reflected on the following month's bill.

❓ Question

Every time I try to map my Cannon home drive on my Windows laptop I end up locked out of my account. How many bad tries does that take, how long am I locked out for, and what am I doing wrong in the Map Network Drive box?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unlock_account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
### Summary of Attempted Actions The agent attempted to diagnose why mapping the Cannon home drive on Windows causes account lockouts by searching the knowledge base for specific guidance on account lockout policies, correct directory path formats, and troubleshooting steps for Windows network drive mapping. ### Best Possible Answer Based on the retrieved documentation from Harvard FAS Research Computing: 1. **How many bad tries?** The documentation does not specify an exact number of failed attempts required to trigger a lockout. It states generally that the account locks after "entering the incorrect password multiple times." 2. **How long is the lockout?** Your account will automatically unlock after approximately **5 – 10 minutes**. 3. **What are you doing wrong?** The most common cause for repeated lockouts when mapping drives is entering the wrong password repeatedly in the "Map Network Drive" dialog. * **Correct Path Format:** Ensure you are using the correct UNC path format for your home directory. Typically, this is `\\cannon.rc.fas.harvard.edu\username` (replace `username` with your Harvard Key). * **Credentials:** If the "Connect using different credentials" option is checked, ensure you are entering your full Harvard Key username (e.g., `username@fas.harvard.edu`) and the correct password. * **VPN:** If you are off-campus, ensure you are connected to the Research Computing VPN before attempting to map the drive. **Recommendation:** Wait for the 5–10 minute lockout period to expire. Then, carefully re-enter your credentials, ensuring the username format is correct. If the issue persists, try mapping the drive via Command Prompt (`net use`) to isolate if it is a Windows GUI caching issue. ### Note on Run Termination This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50 without hitting a stop condition. No further tool calls were made.
✅ Expected Answer
You are almost certainly letting Windows authenticate with your local PC account. In the Map Network Drive dialog you must tick "Connect using different credentials"; if you don't, Windows tries your local PC information and after three failed attempts you are locked out. The lockout expires automatically in about 5 minutes. When prompted for network credentials, prepend your FASRC username with RC\ (for example RC\jharvard) so you authenticate against the RC domain.

Question 83: question_83

❓ Question

I'm on a collaborator's network where I can't run a second VPN client. Can I still get files off the cluster with FileZilla, or is the VPN mandatory for everything?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart_admin.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer-filtering, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You **do not** need a VPN to use FileZilla to transfer files to/from the **login nodes** (Cannon or FASSE). SFTP connections to `login.rc.fas.harvard.edu` (Cannon) or `fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` (FASSE) are accessible from off-campus without a VPN [SFTP file transfer using Filezilla (Mac/Windows/Linux)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer/). However, the VPN **is required** if you need to connect to other FASRC servers beyond the login nodes (e.g., file servers, compute nodes, or internal services) [User Quick Start Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide/). **Key FileZilla settings for off-campus use:** - **Host:** `login.rc.fas.harvard.edu` (Cannon) or `fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` (FASSE) - **Protocol:** SFTP – SSH File Transfer Protocol - **Login Type:** Interactive (so you're prompted for your password and OpenAuth token) - **Transfer tab:** Set "Maximum number of connections" to **1** to avoid repeated token prompts during transfers [SFTP file transfer using Filezilla (Mac/Windows/Linux)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer/) So for simply getting files off the cluster via SFTP to/from your home or project directories on the login nodes, FileZilla works fine without a second VPN client.
✅ Expected Answer
You can still use SFTP. Mounting cluster storage as a drive does require the VPN — cluster storage must be routed through a VPN connection — but SFTP to a login node does not require a FASRC/FAS VPN connection, because login nodes use two-factor authentication. FASRC recommends FileZilla as a cross-platform SFTP client; you need OpenAuth set up plus cluster access and a home directory.

Question 84: question_84

❓ Question

My home dir is full and now rm won't even work — it says 'No space left on device' when I try to delete something. How am I supposed to clean up if I can't delete anything?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a known issue: when your home directory is over quota, the filesystem can't allocate the metadata needed to delete files, so `rm` fails with **"No space left on device."** ### Workaround: Truncate files to zero bytes first Instead of deleting files directly, shrink them to 0 bytes to free up space. Once you're under quota, `rm` will work normally again. **Option 1: `truncate`** ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/bigfile.out ``` You can truncate multiple files at once: ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/file1 ~/path/to/file2 ~/path/to/file3 ``` **Option 2: Redirect `/dev/null`** ```bash cat /dev/null > ~/path/to/bigfile ``` ### Find what's using space Once you have a little breathing room, identify the largest files/directories: ```bash cd ~ du -h --max-depth=1 . ``` Drill down into the largest directories to find specific files to delete. ### Check your usage ```bash df -h ~ ``` ### If `du` and `df` disagree If `df` shows you're over quota but `du` shows less, you may have **sparse files** that aren't accounted for by `du`. Use: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` ### Common culprits - **`~/.local`**: Grows when you run `pip install` outside of virtual environments. Consider renaming it (`mv ~/.local ~/.local.bak`) to reclaim space, then reinstall packages properly using conda/mamba or virtual environments. For more details, see the full guide: [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/)
✅ Expected Answer
Shrink a file to zero bytes instead of unlinking it. Pick a large file you intend to remove and run `truncate -s 0 FILENAME` (you can list several files: `truncate -s 0 FILE1 FILE2 FILE3`), or equivalently redirect the empty device into it with `cat /dev/null > ~/mybig.file`. Once enough space is recovered to get you back under quota, `rm` will work again. Use full paths when deleting to avoid removing the wrong file.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Question 85: question_85

❓ Question

Quota says I'm at my limit but when I add up my folders with du it's way less than that. Which one is lying, and how do I find whatever is eating the space?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Neither is lying — they're just measuring different things. Here's what's going on and how to find the culprit: ## Why `du` and `quota` disagree The most common reason is **sparse files** — files that appear large in `du`'s default output but don't actually consume that much disk space. The filesystem quota tracks actual disk blocks used, while `du` by default shows apparent (logical) size. To see the **actual disk usage** instead of apparent size, run: ```bash cd ~ du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` This shows the logical size of directories and should help you identify which ones are consuming the most space [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). ## Other things that can eat space without showing up in `du` 1. **Deleted files still held open by running processes** — If you deleted a file but a process still has it open, the space isn't freed until the process exits. Check with: ```bash lsof +L1 ``` 2. **Hidden directories** like `~/.local` — This folder can grow large when you run `pip install` outside of virtual environments. Check its size: ```bash du -sh ~/.local ``` 3. **Lab directory group quotas** — If you're on `/n/holylabs`, `/n/netscratch`, or active lab storage, quota is tracked at the **group level**, not just your user level. Your `du` of your own files won't reflect what the group is using. Check group usage with: ```bash quota -g /n/holylabs ``` [Checking quota and usage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/) ## Quick next steps 1. Run `du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 .` in your home directory to find the biggest offenders. 2. If you're in a lab directory, check group quota with `quota -g /n/holylabs` (or your lab path). 3. If you recently deleted files, check for open handles with `lsof +L1`. Want help interpreting the output of any of these commands?
✅ Expected Answer
Neither is lying — you likely have sparse files, whose space is counted by the filesystem's quota check (what `df` reports) but not accounted for properly by `du`. To find them, show the logical size instead: `cd ~` then `du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 .` That points you at the directories responsible.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: why does du show less than quota deleted files still counted
🛠️ Tool Call #4: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #5
Neither is lying — they're just measuring different things. Here's what's going on and how to find the culprit: ## Why `du` and `quota` disagree The most common reason is **sparse files** — files that appear large in `du`'s default output but don't actually consume that much disk space. The filesystem quota tracks actual disk blocks used, while `du` by default shows apparent (logical) size. To see the **actual disk usage** instead of apparent size, run: ```bash cd ~ du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` This shows the logical size of directories and should help you identify which ones are consuming the most space [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). ## Other things that can eat space without showing up in `du` 1. **Deleted files still held open by running processes** — If you deleted a file but a process still has it open, the space isn't freed until the process exits. Check with: ```bash lsof +L1 ``` 2. **Hidden directories** like `~/.local` — This folder can grow large when you run `pip install` outside of virtual environments. Check its size: ```bash du -sh ~/.local ``` 3. **Lab directory group quotas** — If you're on `/n/holylabs`, `/n/netscratch`, or active lab storage, quota is tracked at the **group level**, not just your user level. Your `du` of your own files won't reflect what the group is using. Check group usage with: ```bash quota -g <group_name> /n/holylabs ``` [Checking quota and usage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/) ## Quick next steps 1. Run `du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 .` in your home directory to find the biggest offenders. 2. If you're in a lab directory, check group quota with `quota -g <group> /n/holylabs` (or your lab path). 3. If you recently deleted files, check for open handles with `lsof +L1`. Want help interpreting the output of any of these commands?

Question 86: question_86

❓ Question

openconnect on my Ubuntu box dies right after it authenticates — something about not being able to find a script to configure the tunnel. How do I fix that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openauth, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue you're seeing is a common one: OpenConnect needs a **vpnc-script** to configure the tunnel interface after authentication, and it can't find it by default. Here's how to fix it: ### 1. Install the vpnc-script package On Ubuntu/Debian: ```bash sudo apt-get install vpnc-scripts ``` ### 2. Make sure the script is executable ```bash sudo chmod u+x /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script ``` ### 3. If OpenConnect still can't find it, specify the script path explicitly ```bash openconnect --script /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script https://vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu/ ``` Replace `/etc/vpnc/vpnc-script` with the actual path on your system if it's different. This should resolve the "script not found" error after authentication. For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [Connecting to the FAS RC VPN from Linux clients](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn/).
✅ Expected Answer
OpenConnect needs vpnc-script. Install it if you don't have it (Ubuntu/Debian: `sudo apt-get install vpnc-scripts`; Fedora/CentOS: `sudo dnf install vpnc-script`; Arch: `sudo pacman -S vpnc`), make sure it is executable (e.g. `sudo chmod u+x /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script`), and if OpenConnect still can't find it, pass its location explicitly: `openconnect --script /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script https://vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu/` (substituting the actual path on your system).

Question 88: question_88

❓ Question

I hit Ctrl-C on my srun job to stop it and it just printed some status about the tasks and kept running. How do I actually make it stop?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_launch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/network.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you press **Ctrl-C** in an `srun` job, it sends a `SIGINT` to the tasks, but `srun` itself may not exit immediately — especially if I/O is still pending or tasks are slow to terminate. This is normal Slurm behavior. Here’s how to actually stop it: 1. **Try Ctrl-C again** — sometimes a second interrupt forces `srun` to exit. 2. **Use `scancel`** to kill the job step or job: - Find your job ID (it’s printed when `srun` starts, or you can check with `squeue -u $USER`). - Run `scancel ` to terminate the entire job. - If you only want to kill a specific step, use `scancel -s `. 3. **If you're stuck in a terminal**, you can also try pressing **Ctrl-\** (which sends `SIGQUIT`) — this is a stronger interrupt than `SIGINT` and may force the process to exit. For more details, see the Slurm FAQ on what "srun: Force Terminated job" means [Slurm Workload Manager - Frequently Asked Questions](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).
✅ Expected Answer
That is by design: a single is an escape sequence that reports the state of all tasks associated with the srun command. To kill the job, press twice within one second — that sends SIGINT to all tasks and starts a termination sequence of SIGCONT, SIGTERM and SIGKILL to all spawned tasks. A third terminates srun itself immediately, without waiting for the remote tasks to exit or their I/O to complete. (Signal forwarding does not work at all for asynchronous steps launched with --async; use scancel --signal for those.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

🤖 Assistant Message #4

This is normal Slurm behavior. Here’s how to actually stop it: 1. **Try Ctrl-C again** — sometimes a second interrupt forces `srun` to exit. 2. **Use `scancel`** to kill the job step or job: - Find your job ID (it’s printed when `srun` starts, or you can check with `squeue -u $USER`). - Run `scancel <job_id>` to terminate the entire job. - If you only want to kill a specific step, use `scancel -s <step_id>`. 3. **If you're stuck in a terminal**, you can also try pressing **Ctrl-\** (which sends `SIGQUIT`) — this is a stronger interrupt than `SIGINT` and may force the process to exit. For more details, see the Slurm FAQ on what "srun: Force Terminated job" means [Slurm Workload Manager - Frequently Asked Questions](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).

Question 89: question_89

❓ Question

I asked for several tasks in an interactive srun --pty session, but only one of them shows any output and the rest die instantly. What is --pty doing to the others?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The `--pty` flag in `srun` allocates a pseudo-terminal for **interactive use**, which means it's designed for a single user-facing shell session. When you request multiple tasks with `srun --pty -N -n `, Slurm launches all the tasks, but **only the output from task 0 (the first task) is connected to your terminal**. The other tasks run in the background, but their stdout/stderr are not forwarded to your terminal — they effectively "die instantly" from your perspective because you can't see their output. This is by design: `--pty` is meant for interactive shells, not for multi-task parallel workloads where you want to see output from all tasks. ### What to do instead - **For parallel jobs with output from all tasks**, use `sbatch` (batch mode) or `srun` without `--pty`. Slurm will capture output from all tasks and write it to the job's output files (e.g., `slurm-%j.out`). - **If you need interactivity with multiple tasks**, consider using `salloc` to reserve resources first, then launching tasks with `srun` inside your session, or use a tool like `tmux`/`screen` on each node. For more details, see the Slurm documentation on [srun](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html) and the [FAQ](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).
✅ Expected Answer
--pty runs only task zero under a pseudo terminal. It implicitly sets --error and --output to /dev/null for every task except task zero, which can make those other tasks exit immediately — shells in particular typically exit right away in that situation. --pty also implicitly sets --unbuffered, and it applies to step allocations.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

The other tasks run in the background, but their stdout/stderr are not forwarded to your terminal — they effectively "die instantly" from your perspective because you can't see their output. Question 90: question_90

❓ Question

I added two more #SBATCH lines further down my script, below my module load commands, and Slurm completely ignored them. I also tried #SBATCH --job-name=$MYNAME and it came out literally as $MYNAME. Why?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mathematica, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/template.slurm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/hello.sh.slurm, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/C/Example/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Cpp/Example6/runscript.sh

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Two separate issues here: ### 1. #SBATCH lines ignored when placed below module load commands `#SBATCH` directives are **only recognized when they appear at the very top of the script**, before any other commands (including `#!/bin/bash`, `module load`, or any other executable line). Slurm's `sbatch` parser reads the script header for these directives and ignores any `#SBATCH` lines that appear after the first non-comment, non-directive line. This is by design — `sbatch` parses the script *before* executing it, so directives buried mid-script are never seen. **Fix:** Move all `#SBATCH` lines to the top of your script, immediately after (or before) the shebang line (`#!/bin/bash`), and before any other commands. ### 2. `#SBATCH --job-name=$MYNAME` appearing literally as `$MYNAME` The `#SBATCH` directives are parsed by `sbatch` **before** the shell script is executed. At that point, no shell variable expansion has occurred, so `$MYNAME` is treated as a literal string, not as a variable to be expanded. **Fix:** Use one of these approaches instead: - **Set the job name directly** with a literal string: ```bash #SBATCH --job-name=my_job_name ``` - **Use `sbatch`'s `-J` flag from the command line** (this *does* allow variable expansion since it's evaluated by your shell at submission time): ```bash sbatch -J "$MYNAME" myscript.slurm ``` - **Set the job name inside the script** using `scontrol` (though this is less common and may not work in all Slurm configurations): ```bash #!/bin/bash scontrol update JobId=$SLURM_JOB_ID JobName="$MYNAME" ``` The most common and reliable approach is to pass the job name via `sbatch -J` at submission time, or to hard-code it in the `#SBATCH` directive.
✅ Expected Answer
Both behaviors are documented sbatch parsing rules. Slurm stops reading #SBATCH directives once the first non-comment, non-whitespace line in the script has been reached — so any #SBATCH placed after your module load (or any other command) is never processed. And #SBATCH directives are read directly by Slurm, not by the shell, so shell-specific syntax including variable names is read as literal text, which is why $MYNAME was not expanded. Put all #SBATCH lines at the top of the script, before any commands.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

Question 91: question_91

❓ Question

I ran scontrol hold on a job that was already running because I wanted to pause it, and it just kept going. What did that command actually do to my job, and can I pause a running job at all?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**What `scontrol hold` actually did:** According to the [Slurm scontrol documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html), attempting to hold a running job **will not suspend or cancel it**. Instead, it: - Sets the job's priority to 0 - Updates the job's reason field to indicate it is held - This hold will only take effect **if the job is requeued later** (e.g., after a failure or node failure) So your job continued running normally because the hold command only affects scheduling priority for future requeue events, not the current execution. **Can you pause a running job?** Unfortunately, Slurm does not provide a built-in mechanism to pause or suspend a running job in the way you might expect. The job will continue running until it completes, is cancelled, or is terminated. If you need to temporarily stop a job's execution, your options are limited: - **Cancel the job** with `scancel ` (this terminates it) - **Design pauses within your script** using `sleep` or other blocking commands if you need controlled pauses - For background tasks spawned within a job, you can manage them individually with process control (e.g., `kill -STOP ` to pause a specific process, though this is not a Slurm-level pause) For more details on job management, see the [Slurm FAQ](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Holding a running job does not suspend or cancel it. What it does is set the job's priority to 0 and update the job's reason field, which would hold the job if it were requeued at a later time. Actually pausing a running job is the `suspend` command, and only a privileged user or an account coordinator can suspend jobs (time spent suspended does not count against the job's time limit, and a suspended job that is requeued is placed in a held state). Note also that a hold placed by an ordinary user is a user-hold, while one placed by a privileged user is an admin-hold, and the hold type determines who can `release` it.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I ran scontrol hold on a job that was already running because I wanted to pause it, and it just kept going. What did that command actually do to my job, and can I pause a running job at all?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
**What `scontrol hold` actually did:** According to the [Slurm scontrol documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html), attempting to hold a running job **will not suspend or cancel it**. Instead, it: - Sets the job's priority to 0 - Updates the job's reason field to indicate it is held - This hold will only take effect **if the job is requeued later** (e.g., after a failure or node failure) So your job continued running normally because the hold command only affects scheduling priority for future requeue events, not the current execution. **Can you pause a running job?** Unfortunately, Slurm does not provide a built-in mechanism to pause or suspend a running job in the way you might expect. The job will continue running until it completes, is cancelled, or is terminated. If you need to temporarily stop a job's execution, your options are limited: - **Cancel the job** with `scancel <job_id>` (this terminates it) - **Design pauses within your script** using `sleep` or other blocking commands if you need controlled pauses - For background tasks spawned within a job, you can manage them individually with process control (e.g., `kill -STOP <pid>` to pause a specific process, though this is not a Slurm-level pause) For more details on job management, see the [Slurm FAQ](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).

Question 92: question_92

❓ Question

I overwrote my submit script by accident but the job is still sitting in the queue. Is there any way to get the original script back out of Slurm?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/prolog_epilog.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/selinux.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, **Slurm does not store the original submit script in a retrievable format** once the job is queued or running. Here’s what you need to know: ### What Slurm *does* keep - Slurm stores job metadata (user, partition, resources, environment variables, etc.) in its job accounting database, but **not the script text itself** [Slurm Workload Manager - slurm.conf](https://slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html). - The script is read at submission time and passed to the compute node, but it is not archived by Slurm for later retrieval. ### What you can try 1. **Check your local filesystem** — The script may still exist in your working directory or a backup location. 2. **Check compute node** — If the job is still running, the script may be accessible on the compute node where it’s executing. You can find the node name with `squeue -j -h -o %N`, then `ssh ` and look in the job’s working directory (usually `$SLURM_SUBMIT_DIR` or `$HOME`). 3. **Check version control** — If you use Git, Mercurial, or another VCS, your script may be recoverable from history. 4. **Check backups** — If your home directory or project directory is backed up, you may be able to restore a previous version. ### For the future - Keep your submit scripts in version control (Git). - Consider copying scripts to a backup location before modifying them. - Some sites configure Slurm to archive job scripts, but this is not standard and would need to be explicitly set up by system administrators. If you need help recovering a script from a compute node or checking backups, let me know your job ID and I can guide you through the next steps.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — use `scontrol write batch_script [optional_filename]`. It writes the batch script that was submitted for that job to a file, defaulting to slurm-.sh if you don't give a filename; pass `-` instead of a filename to write it to stdout. Only a privileged user or the owner of the job can retrieve the script.

Question 93: question_93

❓ Question

I want to grab a couple of nodes and sit on them for a while, but I don't want salloc to drop me into a shell that I have to keep open. Can I reserve the resources and then send work at them later from my normal terminal?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/power_save.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/power_save.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can reserve resources in the background and then launch jobs against that allocation later from your normal terminal. The key is to use `salloc` with the `--detach` (or `--no-shell`) option, depending on your Slurm version, or to use `sbatch` to create a "keep-alive" job that holds the nodes. ### Option 1: `salloc --detach` ```bash salloc --nodes=2 --time=02:00:00 --detach ``` This reserves the nodes and returns control to your terminal immediately. You can then use `srun` or `salloc`-aware commands against that allocation. You'll get a job ID back that you can monitor with `squeue`. ### Option 2: `sbatch` keep-alive job Create a simple script that just sleeps for the duration you need: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --nodes=2 #SBATCH --time=02:00:00 sleep 7200 # 2 hours ``` Submit it with `sbatch keepalive.sh`. While it runs, the nodes are yours. You can then submit work to them using `srun --jobid= ` or by using `salloc` with the `--exclusive` flag if needed. ### Running work against the allocation Once the resources are reserved, you can submit work to them using: ```bash srun --jobid= ``` Or if you used `salloc --detach`, you can use `srun` directly in your terminal, and it will use the existing allocation. ### Monitoring Use `squeue -u ` to check the status of your allocation. If you need more specific guidance on your Slurm version or partition constraints, let me know!
✅ Expected Answer
Use `salloc --no-shell`. It exits immediately after allocating the resources without running a command, but the Slurm job is still created and stays active, owning the allocated resources for as long as it lives — you get a Slurm job id with no associated processes or tasks. You can then run work against it by passing `--jobid=` to srun. The job is still subject to normal constraints such as time limits, so it will eventually end on its own, or you can end it early with scancel.

Question 95: question_95

❓ Question

Our compute nodes have hyperthreading turned on (2 threads per core). If my job asks for 3 tasks, does Slurm hand me exactly 3 hyperthreads, and could another user's job end up sharing a core with mine?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Python-Multiprocessing-Tutorial/cpu-count.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
I'm sorry, but the conversation history has grown too large for me to process. Please start a new conversation to continue.
✅ Expected Answer
With hyperthreading enabled a "CPU" in Slurm means a hyperthread (without it, a CPU is a core), but Slurm still generally allocates resources to jobs at the level of a whole core. A job requesting three tasks on a node with ThreadsPerCore=2 is therefore allocated two full cores (four threads), not three threads. Two different jobs will not share a core unless the partition's OverSubscribe configuration parameter allows it. You can check ThreadsPerCore with "scontrol show node". The exception is the atypical configuration where the admin sets SelectTypeParameters=CR_CPU with only a CPU count per node (no socket/core/thread spec) — then each thread is scheduled independently as a CPU.

Question 96: question_96

❓ Question

My MPI run blows up about half a minute after one rank finishes early — the rest of the ranks were still working. Can I stop Slurm from tearing the whole thing down that fast?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart_admin.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/troubleshoot.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to search the knowledge base for solutions regarding Slurm terminating jobs prematurely when an MPI rank fails or exits early. The searches focused on Slurm's behavior regarding early job exits and MPI termination signals. **Summary of Attempt:** The agent queried for documentation on how Slurm handles job termination when one rank finishes while others are still running, specifically looking for configuration options to prevent immediate job cancellation. **Answer:** Yes, you can prevent Slurm from tearing down the job immediately. By default, Slurm sends a signal (usually SIGTERM or SIGKILL) to the entire job step when the first rank exits or fails. To allow the remaining ranks to finish their work or handle the error gracefully, you should configure the job step to not terminate on early exit. 1. **Use `--kill-on-bad-exit=0`**: This is the most direct solution. Add `--kill-on-bad-exit=0` to your `sbatch` or `srun` command. This tells Slurm not to kill the job step if a rank exits with a non-zero status. The job will remain active until all ranks exit or the job time limit is reached. 2. **Use `--exit-on-error=0`**: Similar to the above, this prevents Slurm from terminating the job if any rank exits with an error. 3. **Handle MPI Errors in Code**: Ensure your MPI application handles the early exit of a rank gracefully (e.g., by detecting the failure and calling `MPI_Abort` only when appropriate, or by continuing if the logic allows). If one rank crashes, the others might hang waiting for communication; using `--kill-on-bad-exit=0` gives them time to detect the issue and shut down cleanly. **Note:** The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50, so further detailed troubleshooting or specific Slurm version configurations were not explored.
✅ Expected Answer
That is srun's default behavior, not a crash: srun monitors when tasks exit and by default kills the job 30 seconds after the first task exits (producing "srun: First task exited 30s ago" followed by "srun Job Failed"). Use srun's --wait= option to either lengthen the timeout period or disable the timeout altogether.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Question 97: question_97

❓ Question

Why does sacct come back empty for a job I ran last Tuesday, but show it just fine as soon as I pass the job ID with -j?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sattach.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common SLURM behavior caused by **default filtering** in `sacct`. ### Why it happens By default, `sacct` only shows jobs for the **current user** and often applies a **time window filter** (typically only showing jobs from the last few days). If your job from last Tuesday falls outside that default time window, or if there's a user/account filtering issue, `sacct` will return empty results. When you use `sacct -j `, you're explicitly requesting that specific job, which bypasses the default filters and shows the job regardless of time window or user restrictions. ### How to fix it 1. **Remove the time filter** by using the `--all` flag: ```bash sacct --all ``` 2. **Or specify a broader time range**: ```bash sacct --starttime="2024-01-01" ``` 3. **Check your user/account context**: ```bash sacct -u $USER ``` 4. **For your specific job**, you can always use: ```bash sacct -j ``` The key takeaway is that `sacct`'s default behavior is conservative—it only shows recent jobs for your user. Use `--all` or specify explicit time ranges when querying older jobs. For more details, see the [sacct documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html).
✅ Expected Answer
sacct's default search window changes depending on which options you use. With neither --jobs nor --state specified, --starttime defaults to Midnight and --endtime defaults to Now, so a job from last week falls outside the window and nothing is returned. As soon as you specify --jobs, --starttime defaults to Epoch 0 (with --endtime still Now), so the old job is found. To list older jobs without a job ID, pass an explicit -S/--starttime. Running sacct with -v/--verbose prints the time window actually in use.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Question 98: question_98

❓ Question

sacct -X shows my completed job but every memory and CPU utilization column reads 0. Is our accounting broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Cpp/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/job_exit_code.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sh5util.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, the `-X` flag in `sacct` is **not** a standard accounting flag — it is actually a flag for the **`sh5util`** utility (used for extracting data from Slurm H5 profiling files), not for `sacct` itself. See the [Slurm sh5util documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sh5util.html) which shows `sacct -X -j 5077 --format=JobId,JobIdRaw` in an example context related to H5 profiling extraction. However, looking at the [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/) page from FASRC, we see examples like: ```bash sacct -j --format=JobID,JobName,MaxRSS,Elapsed,nodelist -X ``` This suggests that in some Slurm versions or configurations, `-X` may be a valid `sacct` option. According to the [Slurm sacct documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html), the `-X` flag is used to **exclude job steps** from the output, showing only the top-level job record. **Why memory and CPU utilization columns read 0:** When you use `sacct -X`, you're only seeing the **aggregate job-level record**, not the step-level records where actual resource utilization (CPU time, memory used) is typically collected. The job-level record often shows zeros for utilization fields because: 1. **Resource accounting happens at the step level** — Slurm's `acct_gather` plugins collect memory and CPU usage per job step, not per the parent job record. 2. **The `-X` flag suppresses step records** — so you're only seeing the parent job, which may not have utilization data populated. **To see actual utilization, try:** ```bash sacct -j --format=JobID,JobName,MaxRSS,AveCPU,TotalCPU,Elapsed ``` Or use the `seff` command, which is designed to show job efficiency including memory and CPU utilization: ```bash seff ``` As shown in the [FASRC training examples](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md), `seff` provides a clean summary like: ``` CPU Utilized: 00:02:10 CPU Efficiency: 95.59% of 00:02:16 core-walltime Memory Utilized: 25.27 GB Memory Efficiency: 63.17% of 40.00 GB ``` **Bottom line:** Your accounting is likely not broken — you're just querying at the wrong granularity. Drop the `-X` flag or use `seff` to see actual resource utilization.
✅ Expected Answer
Not necessarily. -X/--allocations only shows statistics relevant to the job allocation itself and does not take job steps into consideration, and without including steps the utilization statistics for the allocation are reported as zero. Drop -X (or query the steps) to see real usage. Separately, which metrics exist at all depends on the jobacct_gather plugin in use — for example jobacct_gather/cgroup with cgroup/v2 cannot provide virtual-memory metrics and shows 0 for those fields.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Based on the documentation, the `-X` flag in `sacct` is **not** a standard accounting flag — it is actually a flag for the **`sh5util`** utility (used for extracting data from Slurm H5 profiling files), not for `sacct` itself. See the [Slurm sh5util documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sh5util.html) which shows `sacct -X -j 5077 --format=JobId,JobIdRaw` in an example context related to H5 profiling extraction. However, looking at the [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/) page from FASRC, we see examples like: ```bash sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,JobName,MaxRSS,Elapsed,nodelist -X ``` This suggests that in some Slurm versions or configurations, `-X` may be a valid `sacct` option. According to the [Slurm sacct documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html), the `-X` flag is used to **exclude job steps** from the output, showing only the top-level job record. **Why memory and CPU utilization columns read 0:** When you use `sacct -X`, you're only seeing the **aggregate job-level record**, not the step-level records where actual resource utilization (CPU time, memory used) is typically collected. The job-level record often shows zeros for utilization fields because: 1. **Resource accounting happens at the step level** — Slurm's `acct_gather` plugins collect memory and CPU usage per job step, not per the parent job record. 2. **The `-X` flag suppresses step records** — so you're only seeing the parent job, which may not have utilization data populated. **To see actual utilization, try:** ```bash sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,JobName,MaxRSS,AveCPU,TotalCPU,Elapsed ``` Or use the `seff` command, which is designed to show job efficiency including memory and CPU utilization: ```bash seff <jobid> ``` As shown in the [FASRC training examples](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md), `seff` provides a clean summary like: ``` CPU Utilized: 00:02:10 CPU Efficiency: 95.59% of 00:02:16 core-walltime Memory Utilized: 25.27 GB Memory Efficiency: 63.17% of 40.00 GB ``` **Bottom line:** Your accounting is likely not broken — you're just querying at the wrong granularity. Drop the `-X` flag or use `seff` to see actual resource utilization.

Question 99: question_99

❓ Question

Is there a way to keep an eye on just my own jobs in the queue, refreshing every 30 seconds, without typing my username or wrapping the command in a shell loop?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/EP/Example1/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gnu-screen, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/training-materials

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can use the Unix `watch` command to automatically refresh `squeue` every 30 seconds, and since `squeue` defaults to showing **your** jobs, you don't need to specify your username: ```bash watch -n 30 squeue ``` This will: - Run `squeue` (which shows only the current user's jobs by default) - Refresh the output every 30 seconds (`-n 30`) - Keep running until you press `Ctrl+C` If you want to see only running or only pending jobs, you can add the `-t` flag: ```bash watch -n 30 squeue -t RUNNING # only running jobs watch -n 30 squeue -t PENDING # only pending jobs ``` This approach avoids wrapping the command in a shell loop and doesn't require typing your username, since `squeue` defaults to the current user. See the [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/) page for more details on `squeue` usage.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — run `squeue --me -i 30`. The --me option is equivalent to --user=, and -i/--iterate= makes squeue repeatedly gather and report the requested information at the interval specified (printing a time stamp with the header by default). Note the man page's PERFORMANCE warning: executing squeue sends a remote procedure call to slurmctld, so do not run squeue from loops in shell scripts or other programs, and keep calls to the minimum necessary — enough of them can degrade slurmctld and even cause a denial of service.

Question 100: question_100

❓ Question

I want a one-line-per-partition view of how many nodes are busy versus free, but sinfo keeps giving me several rows per partition. What do I run, and how do I read the counts it prints?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Run **`sinfo --summarize`** (or `sinfo -s`). ### Why this works By default, `sinfo` prints **one row per partition–state combination** (e.g., one row for `idle`, another for `alloc`, etc.), which is why you see multiple rows per partition. The `--summarize` flag collapses everything into **one row per partition**, aggregating the node counts across all states. ### How to read the output A typical line looks like: ``` PARTITION AVAIL TIMELIMIT NODES STATE NODELIST debug up 2:00:00 4 idle node[01-04] debug up 2:00:00 2 alloc node[05-06] ``` With `--summarize`, you'll instead see something like: ``` PARTITION AVAIL TIMELIMIT NODES TOTAL ALLOC IDLE OTHER debug up 2:00:00 6 6 2 4 0 ``` - **TOTAL** — total nodes in the partition - **ALLOC** — nodes currently allocated (busy) - **IDLE** — nodes available and not allocated (free) - **OTHER** — nodes in any other state (e.g., down, mixed, reserved) So to quickly see busy vs. free nodes per partition, just run: ```bash sinfo --summarize ``` See the `sinfo` man page for full details on the `--summarize` option [Slurm Workload Manager - sinfo](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Run `sinfo -s` (--summarize), which lists only a partition state summary with no node state details (internally it uses the format "%#P %.5a %.10l %.16F %N"). In that output the NODES(A/I/O/T) column is the count of nodes by state in the form allocated/idle/other/total — so, for example, 2/6/0/8 means 2 allocated, 6 idle, 0 other, 8 total.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Question 101: question_101

❓ Question

Several nodes are offline and I want to see the note the admins left about why. Which sinfo option shows those notes, and how much of each note do I actually get to see?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the Slurm documentation, the option you want is **`-R`** (or **`--reason`**). ### How to use it Run: ```bash sinfo -R ``` This reports only nodes that are down, drained, or draining, along with the **REASON** field that admins set to explain why the node is unavailable. ### How much of the note do you see? The documentation shows the `REASON` column in the output, but it does not specify a hard character limit for the note text itself. In practice, Slurm stores the full reason string, but the terminal width and column formatting may truncate very long notes visually. If you need the complete text, you can use the `--format` option with the `%E` field (which represents the node reason) to control the output explicitly, for example: ```bash sinfo -o "%N %E" ``` This lets you see the node name and its reason without the default column width constraints. For more details, see the [sinfo man page](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Use `sinfo -R` (--list-reasons). It lists the reasons nodes are in the down, drained, fail or failing state — the administrator-supplied "reason" string — but it displays only the first 20 characters of the reason field, along with the list of nodes carrying that reason. Adding -l (--long) also includes the current node state in the output.

Question 102: question_102

❓ Question

I compiled my binary in my home directory on the login node and the job can't find it once it lands on a compute node. Does the scheduler copy my files out to the nodes for me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/hdf5_profile_user_guide.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart_admin.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the scheduler **does not** copy your files from your home directory to the compute nodes for you. Here's what's happening: 1. **Home directories are shared** — Your `$HOME` is typically mounted across all nodes via a shared filesystem (like NFS), so your compiled binary *should* be accessible on the compute nodes if it's in your home directory. 2. **Working directory matters** — By default, SLURM sets the job's working directory to the directory from which you submitted the job (usually your home directory or wherever you ran `sbatch`). Your job will look for files relative to that working directory. 3. **Common issue** — If you compiled your binary in your home directory but your job script is trying to find it in a different location (or if you're using a relative path that doesn't resolve correctly), the job won't find it. **What to do:** - Use **absolute paths** to your binary in your job script, e.g., `/n/holylabs/YOUR_LAB/username/my_binary` or `~/my_binary` - Or, better yet, compile your code in a **scratch directory** (`/n/netscratch`) or a **project directory** (`/n/holylabs/`) rather than your home directory, since home directories are not intended for heavy I/O or compilation - Make sure your job script's `cd` (if any) or working directory matches where the binary lives If you're still having trouble, share the relevant part of your job script and I can help debug the path issue.
✅ Expected Answer
No. Slurm does not automatically migrate executable or data files to the nodes allocated to a job — the files must already exist on local disk on those nodes or on a global file system such as NFS or Lustre. If you do want a copy on the compute nodes' local storage, use sbcast, which transfers a file to local disk on the allocated nodes using Slurm's hierarchical communications (e.g. `sbcast a.out /tmp/joe.a.out`, then run it with srun and delete it from local storage afterwards).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

Question 103: question_103

❓ Question

I ran scancel -s KILL on my job. The processes died, but the job is still sitting there holding its nodes. Why didn't it go away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/troubleshoot.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No answer generated by the agent.
✅ Expected Answer
Because sending a signal value of KILL to an entire job cancels the job's active job steps but does not cancel the job itself, and cancelling a job step never terminates the job — the job must be cancelled to release its resource allocation. To actually cancel it, invoke scancel with no --signal option: that sends a SIGCONT to all steps to wake them up, then a SIGTERM, waits the KillWait duration defined in slurm.conf, and finally sends SIGKILL if they have not terminated.

Question 104: question_104

❓ Question

Can I wipe out all of my queued-but-not-yet-started jobs in one partition with a single command, without touching the ones already running and without listing job IDs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can cancel all of your **queued (PENDING)** jobs in a specific partition with a single `scancel` command, without listing individual job IDs and without affecting jobs that are already running. The command is: ```bash scancel -u $USER -p --state=PENDING ``` ### What this does: - `-u $USER` — targets only your jobs - `-p ` — limits the scope to the specified partition - `--state=PENDING` — only matches jobs that are queued (not RUNNING, COMPLETED, etc.) This will **not** touch jobs that are already running, because they are in the `RUNNING` state, not `PENDING`. ### Example: ```bash scancel -u $USER -p regular --state=PENDING ``` This cancels all of your queued jobs in the `regular` partition. For reference, see the `scancel` documentation at [Slurm Workload Manager - scancel](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — filter scancel by state, owner and partition, e.g. `scancel --state=PENDING --user=bob --partition=debug` (use --me instead of --user to mean your own jobs). The -t/--state filter accepts PENDING, RUNNING or SUSPENDED, and when multiple filters are supplied only the jobs satisfying all of the filtering options are signaled, so running jobs in that partition are left alone.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

🤖 Assistant Message #4
Yes — you can cancel all of your **queued (PENDING)** jobs in a specific partition with a single `scancel` command, without listing individual job IDs and without affecting jobs that are already running. The command is: ```bash scancel -u $USER -p <partition> --state=PENDING ``` ### What this does: - `-u $USER` — targets only your jobs - `-p <partition>` — limits the scope to the specified partition - `--state=PENDING` — only matches jobs that are queued (not RUNNING, COMPLETED, etc.) This will **not** touch jobs that are already running, because they are in the `RUNNING` state, not `PENDING`. ### Example: ```bash scancel -u $USER -p regular --state=PENDING ``` This cancels all of your queued jobs in the `regular` partition. For reference, see the `scancel` documentation at [Slurm Workload Manager - scancel](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html).

Question 105: question_105

❓ Question

The Slurm website's manual pages describe a much newer release than the one our cluster actually runs. Where can I get documentation that matches our older version?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/documentation.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/big_sys.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart_admin.html, slurm.schedmd.com/documentation.html, slurm.schedmd.com, slurm.schedmd.com/programmer_guide.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The main Slurm documentation site ([slurm.schedmd.com](https://slurm.schedmd.com/)) currently displays docs for version 26.05, but it notes that documentation for older versions is available elsewhere. To find docs matching your cluster's older Slurm release: 1. **Check the Slurm documentation page** — it explicitly states that older version docs are available at a separate location. Visit [https://slurm.schedmd.com/documentation.html](https://slurm.schedmd.com/documentation.html) for a link to archived versions. 2. **Use the SchedMD download page** — older Slurm releases and their corresponding documentation can be found at [https://www.schedmd.com/download-slurm/](https://www.schedmd.com/download-slurm/). 3. **Check your cluster's Slurm version** — run `sinfo --version` or `scontrol --version` to confirm the exact version your cluster is running, then look for the matching archived docs. If you'd like, I can help you determine your cluster's Slurm version and locate the specific archived documentation for it.
✅ Expected Answer
The online documentation set on slurm.schedmd.com is for Slurm version 26.05. Documentation for older versions of Slurm is distributed with the Slurm source itself, or can be found in SchedMD's documentation archive at https://slurm.schedmd.com/archive/.

Question 106: question_106

❓ Question

Which SLURM partition on FASRC Cannon should I submit GPU jobs to, and what flag do I use to request GPUs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, slurm.schedmd.com/gres.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For general GPU jobs on FASRC Cannon, submit to the **`gpu`** partition and request GPUs using the **`--gpus`** flag (e.g., `--gpus=1` for one GPU) [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/). Example submission script: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` If you want to test GPU resources interactively, you can use the **`gpu_test`** partition with `--gres=gpu:n` (where *n* = 1–8) [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs). For backfill/idle GPU nodes, use the **`gpu_requeue`** partition, and you can constrain by GPU model or compute capability (e.g., `--constraint=cc3.7`) [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/).
✅ Expected Answer
Use the gpu partition (or gpu_test for short test jobs). Request GPUs with --gres=gpu:N in your SLURM submit script, e.g. --gres=gpu:1.

Question 107: question_107

❓ Question

Where on the FASRC Cannon filesystem should I store large datasets that I don't need backed up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-cluster-storage-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For large datasets you don't need backed up, you have two main options on Cannon: 1. **`/n/netscratch`** – A large, high-performance shared scratch filesystem. Each lab gets 50TB, but it has a **90-day retention policy** and files may be purged at any time, especially if space is low. It is **not backed up** [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). 2. **`/n/holylabs`** – Your lab's holylabs directory. It provides 4TB, allows longer retention, and is also **not backed up** (though snapshots may exist) [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage). **Which to choose:** - Use **`/n/netscratch`** for high I/O jobs and transient data you can afford to lose after 90 days. - Use **`/n/holylabs`** if you need longer retention but still don't require backups. If you later need backups or more space, FASRC offers paid storage options like Compute Storage and Lab Storage — see the [Data Storage Workflow](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm) for details.
✅ Expected Answer
Use /n/holyscratch01// for fast scratch storage. It is not backed up and is periodically purged of older files.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Question 108: question_108

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed with an out-of-memory error. How do I diagnose what the actual memory peak was, and how do I request more memory for the next submission?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to diagnose the memory peak and request more memory for your next job: ### Diagnosing the memory peak 1. **Use `jobstats` on a completed job** Run `jobstats ` to see how much memory your job actually used. This is the most accurate way to determine your memory peak. 2. **Important caveat** If your job exited with `OUT_OF_MEMORY`, the memory usage recorded by Slurm will be inaccurate. To get a true measurement, you need a job that **completes successfully** so Slurm can record the actual peak. Start by requesting a generous amount of memory, let the job finish, then check `jobstats` to see the real peak. 3. **For multi-node jobs** `jobstats` is particularly useful here because it shows memory usage per node. Note that Slurm's `--mem` limit is the maximum memory used on **any one node**, so if tasks aren't evenly distributed (e.g., without `--ntasks-per-node`), your job could show different values on different runs. ### Requesting more memory Add this line to your job submission script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the maximum memory in MB, or use `G` for gigabytes. For example: ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` ### Recommended workflow 1. **Start large**: Job slots on average have about 4000 MB per core, so request a generous amount initially. 2. **Run the job** to completion. 3. **Check `jobstats `** to see the actual memory peak. 4. **Adjust `--mem`** to something slightly larger than what `jobstats` reports (since `--mem` is a hard upper limit). 5. **Resubmit** with the tuned value. Smaller memory requests are easier for the scheduler to place, so once you know your actual peak, request only what you need.
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j --format=JobID,MaxRSS,ReqMem to see the peak memory the job used (MaxRSS) versus what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script, increase the memory request via --mem=G (whole-job) or --mem-per-cpu=G (per-task), aiming a bit above the observed MaxRSS so future variations don't kill the job again.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 109: question_109

❓ Question

I want to run a multi-node MPI program on Cannon that uses one GPU per node. What does the SLURM submit script need to specify, and how do I launch the MPI ranks?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_mpi.cu, slurm.schedmd.com/gres.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise4/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/PyTorch/slurm_ddp_tp_fsdp.sh

⚠️ Status: PARTIALLY CORRECT (1/2 sources found)

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's what you need to specify in your SLURM submit script and how to launch MPI ranks for a **multi-node, one-GPU-per-node** job on Cannon: --- ### 1. SLURM script directives ```bash #SBATCH --nodes=2 # number of nodes #SBATCH --ntasks-per-node=1 # one MPI rank per node #SBATCH --gpus-per-node=1 # one GPU per node #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=1 # CPUs to reserve for the rank #SBATCH --partition=gpu # GPU-capable partition #SBATCH --time=00:10:00 # walltime ``` Key points: - **`--ntasks-per-node=1`** ensures exactly one MPI rank per node. - **`--gpus-per-node=1`** assigns one GPU to each rank. - **`--nodes`** sets the total number of nodes (and thus total MPI ranks). See the FASRC example script: [slurm_ddp_tp_fsdp.sh](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/PyTorch/slurm_ddp_tp_fsdp.sh). --- ### 2. Environment variables for distributed training Each rank needs to know the global rank and the master node: ```bash export MASTER_ADDR=$(scontrol show hostnames | head -n 1) export MASTER_PORT=39591 export RANK=$SLURM_PROCID export WORLD_SIZE=$(($SLURM_NNODES * $SLURM_NTASKS_PER_NODE)) ``` - **`MASTER_ADDR`**: hostname of the first node (rank 0). - **`RANK`**: global rank (`$SLURM_PROCID` is 0 on the first node, 1 on the second, etc.). - **`WORLD_SIZE`**: total number of ranks across all nodes. --- ### 3. Launching MPI ranks Use **`srun`** to launch your MPI program: ```bash srun --ntasks-per-node=$SLURM_NTASKS_PER_NODE \ python -u your_mpi_program.py ``` `srun` automatically spawns one task per node (as specified by `--ntasks-per-node=1`), and each task gets one GPU. Your program should select the GPU using the local rank (e.g., `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` is set by SLURM to the assigned GPU). --- ### Summary | Step | What to do | |---|---| | **Script** | Set `--nodes`, `--ntasks-per-node=1`, `--gpus-per-node=1` | | **Env vars** | Export `MASTER_ADDR`, `MASTER_PORT`, `RANK`, `WORLD_SIZE` | | **Launch** | Use `srun` to start your MPI program | For a concrete example, see the FASRC training code: [Multi-Node MPI + NCCL example](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_mpi.cu).
✅ Expected Answer
In your submit script: choose a GPU-enabled partition (e.g. gpu), set --nodes= with --ntasks-per-node=1 and --gres=gpu:1 (per-node GPU request), load the MPI and CUDA modules with module load. Launch the program with srun (not mpirun directly) so SLURM places one rank per node with the bound GPU; e.g. srun --mpi=pmix ./my_program. Use sinfo and scontrol show partition gpu to confirm node configuration before submitting.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface
